Ask a Local: Where to Go in Nairobi According to Runner Emily Chepkor

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

"Nairobi is a city best understood in motion," says Emily Chepkor, founder of We Run Nairobi, the city's biggest and most popular run club. She's from a small town in Kenya, Kapsabet, that has produced more world-class runners per square mile than anywhere else on earth. While the country is known worldwide for its running heritage, Nairobi hadn't quite caught up to the hype until Chepkor came along.

She runs as a hobby, and is a 12-time marathoner (and two-time Boston qualifier) who felt that, though Kenya's running reputation was unmatched internationally, the runner lifestyle itself wasn't accessible to everyday Kenyans—elite athletes only. She set out to change that and bring running closer to all Nairobians, starting with a weekly run club that began with just her and three friends. It slowly grew through word of mouth and especially TikTok, into something bigger, built on a simple idea: no registration, no fees, you just show up and run alongside Emily.

That run has become a defining weekly ritual for Nairobians, drawing over 1,200 people every Saturday morning. It's now etched into the city's identity. The same way New York is known for its bagels, Nairobi is known for the We Run Nairobi neighborhood runs, a cultural landmark for locals and tourists alike.

Below, Emily tells us about some of her favorite things to do in the city she calls home.

Karura Forest is “without a doubt” the best place to run in Nairobi, if you ask Emily.

"Karura Forest, without a doubt, is the most scenic route in the city”, she says. “I run the Sigiria side, through Gate F; it's quieter, more canopied. My dog Biscuit and I do the 10-kilometer loop most Sundays, crossing little rivers every kilometer. If you want something gentler, the 5-kilometer route is just as lovely." Her Saturday club runs the same way: "We're completely pace-agnostic. First-timers run next to marathoners".

Karura is the second largest urban forest in the world after the Tijuca Forest in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, and provides the perfect place for an outdoor run.

“Kengele's is always a safe bet whatever's on, whether it's World Cup, rugby, athletics, you'll find a good crowd," says Chepkor. "What I love is how the embassies get involved: around big sporting events, they'll partner with venues across the city to host public watch parties. I'll never forget the British High Commission's watch party in Karura Forest, the day Sebastian Sawe broke the two-hour marathon barrier. The whole country was locked in, and experiencing that moment surrounded by hundreds of people felt incredibly special.”

The McMillan Memorial Library is one of her favorite places for some peace and quiet, and some history.

“The McMillan Memorial Library is a perfect little time capsule of Nairobi, architecturally and literally—it's currently being renovated by Book Bunk, founded by two Kenyan women committed to restoring our public libraries," she says. "I also love the Nairobi Gallery, home to Point Zero, the spot from which all distances in Kenya are measured.”

“Tigoni is a small town only 30 to 40 minutes from Nairobi, but it feels much farther away. The city gives way to rolling tea plantations and narrow country roads. I start at Brackenhurst with coffee from Muna Tree Cafe on the terrace, then rent a bicycle and ride through the plantations, stopping wherever the view is too good to pass.” Back at Brackenhurst, she'll spread a blanket on the lawn to read, maybe doze off, then finish with tennis or a walk through the Indigenous forest. "It's the itinerary I'm proudest to take an out-of-towner on," Chepkor says.

"Sigiria Coffee House is my default—small, intimate, and full of dogs on Sunday mornings. On tennis mornings it's Waitabit, same easy vibe." For souvenirs, she sends visitors to two spots near each other: "Spinners Web has beautiful rugs, a café, and a used-books library. Just up the road, Tribal Gallery has grander pieces—restored Swahili doors, oversized mirrors—alongside smaller suitcase-friendly finds like ceramics and jewelry."

"Fig & Olive in Tigoni is part bodega, part restaurant, with a lawn where no one blinks if you swap your chair for the grass. In the city, it's the Tin Roof Café in Karen—moving from table to grass and back, especially post-run, and the salad bar is iconic. For something dressier that still softens outdoors, Matteo's: white tablecloths, al fresco seating, and little intimate corners I'm still discovering."

The Talisman Restaurant is a Nairobi stalwart.

Scoring a place by the fireplace is a coup.

"The Talisman Restaurant is a Nairobi stalwart. Scoring a corner table by the fireplace on a chilly evening is a date-night dream. For something more stylish, Inti has skyline views in every direction—some Friday nights it turns into an intimate DJ set." For the rest of the evening, she splits the city in two: “If you're after energy, Geco, alive with Nairobi's music scene, especially on Wednesdays. For actual conversation, Cave-à-Manger has an expansive wine list and keeps the music in the background.”

"Toi Market, with one caveat: Nairobi isn't really a vintage city in the curated, boutique sense— we're much more of an open-air, flea market vibe, with thousands of stalls. Toi is sprawling, busy, and a little chaotic. You'll need patience and a willingness to dig, but if you put in the time, you'll almost always come away with something great for a fraction of what you'd pay elsewhere."

“Nai Nami is properly off the beaten track; it's a walking tour of Nairobi's CBD led by former street children. You see downtown Nairobi through the eyes of people who know it intimately, and leave with a completely different understanding of the city. The Alliance Française is an unexpected answer, but it's one of Nairobi's great cultural hubs—plays, concerts, exhibitions, most of which are free, and the original stomping ground of Sauti Sol, our biggest boy band.”

A view of the city from Nairobi National Park

"Nairobi is the only city in the world with a national park so if you’ve only got one day, make it a wildlife day. I’d go for a game drive through Nairobi National Park at sunrise, then the elephant feeding at Sheldrick Wildlife Trust, and the Giraffe Centre to finish. Afterwards, a slow lunch nearby at either Cultiva, Talisman, or Matteo's. "

"I'd highly recommend coming to a We Run Nairobi Saturday. One of my favorite traditions is that we 'brand' every first-timer with a little sticker, so everyone knows it's your first Saturday. I'll encourage people to say hello, ask where you're visiting from, and recommend a coffee spot. You're moving together with actual Nairobians, in neighborhoods where we actually live. You're not being shown the city, you get to experience it alongside the people who call it home.”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Opinion: Liverpool must raid Premier League rivals for £40m defender

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Liverpool are currently fifth in the Premier League table.

And, while that may not seem all that bad, Arne Slot was relieved of his duties as the Reds' manager for finishing in fifth place last season.

Fortunately, Liverpool still managed to qualify for the Champions League which is a competition that they seem to be doing fairly well in.

However, it's been made more than clear from their performances against other teams such as Manchester City and Arsenal that some new additions are needed.

Without these, there's no way that they'll be able to get back to the top of the Premier League table which, at the end of the day, is going to be their main goal.

So, now that the opportunity for Liverpool to bolster their defence has emerged, it's an option that they seriously need to take.

Liverpool are interested in signing El Hadji Malick Diouf from Brentford

Recently, left-back has emerged as a big problem for Liverpool.

Both Kostas Tsimikas and Milos Kerkez have failed to meet expectations with both players performing rather poorly.

Before the season properly kicked off, fans believed that midfield was going to be the main issue that the Reds would need to solve in January.

However, thanks to the recent displays put on show by the aforementioned duo, some now believe that a new left-back should be on top of their priority list.

Many different options have emerged but, recently, an update from Senegalese journalist Khadim Diakhate has linked the Reds with Brentford's El Hadji Malick Diouf.

"Transfer Market: Chelsea and Liverpool interested in El Hadji Malick Diouf after his arrival at Brentford," he wrote.

"Chelsea and Liverpool are interested in El Hadji Malick Diouf ahead of the next summer transfer window. According to our information, the two English clubs have already made contact with the agent of the Senegalese left-back.

"Having arrived at Brentford last summer, the Lion of the Teranga is already catching the eye of the Premier League's big clubs."

Liverpool must sign El Hadji Malick Diouf as their new left-back

Although Diouf may not have ended the season all that well with West Ham last term, his performances from earlier in the campaign proved that he had it in him to be an important player.

Obviously, he's still only 21 years old so there's plenty of time for him to improve but, he's already a solid left-back.

His attacking output is also fairly impressive as, whenever he gets the chance, he's always pushing up the pitch and trying to create chances.

For Iraola's system, he's literally the perfect full-back and, even though Kerkez was also seen that way, things just haven't quite clicked for the Hungarian at Liverpool this term.

Tsimikas' contract is going to expire at the end of the current season so, with no renewal yet in sight, he's going to have to be replaced soon anyway.

So, getting someone such as Diouf in during the January transfer window makes a lot of sense as it would allow him to get adjusted to life at Liverpool and to their system.

No price tag was mentioned in the tweet from Diakhate but, since the Bees spent £40 million on him in the summer, they'll at least want that back.

Jordan Firstman on Club Slang, Directing His Friends, and the Perfect Date Night

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Jordan Firstman’s directorial debut, Club Kid, was the talk of Cannes this year, earning a seven-minute standing ovation and starting a bidding war.  “I had a funny LA tarot card reader right before Cannes. I hadn’t sold the movie until Cannes and she was like, ‘This card is A24, and this card is Neon. If you go with A24, I think there’s a multi-picture deal happening,’” Jordan Firstman shared with Chloe on The Run-Through.

The film is out Friday and features Cara Delevigne, Saturn Risin9, and a cast of extras found on Grindr and in the local nightlife scene. But one of the standouts is newcomer Reggie Absoholm, who plays Arlo, the long-lost 10-year-old son that Peter (played by Jordan) finds out he has. “I do stuff to, like, cringe [Reggie] out ’cause I just like it. I do feel like a dad to him sometimes, and, like, it’s fun. I feel like that’s what made him comfortable on set was just being able to make fun of me.”

In his conversation with Chloe, Jordan shares why he chose to center New Yrok for a story about friendship and club culture, gives her a quick rundown on drug lingo, and talks about being newly in love.

Listen to the full conversation here—and read an abridged version below.

Chloe Malle: Jordan, welcome to the podcast. Thank you so much for being here.

Jordan Firstman: Thank you for having me.

I’ve been getting a lot of press alerts about your outfits, so I feel like you’ve been really on a tour.

I’m really glad to hear that because I’ve had some looks I’m proud of.

What was the first fashion show you ever went to?

During the pandemic, I was the virtual host of a Thom Browne fashion show.

I played a newscaster, and that was my first fashion-related thing. I think the first show I went to was randomly Vetements. They sat me next to Edward Enninful and someone else fancy, and I was living. But it was exciting to be at a fashion show.

It is exciting because it’s like theater for people with ADD. You sit, you wait, you’re excited, and then 12 minutes later, it’s done.

And you’re like, “Oh, I witnessed this cultural experience, and I didn’t have to sit through two and a half hours and an intermission.”

It’s true. I didn’t have to watch a whole feature film. You’re so mad at me for making a movie. You’re like, “Why wasn’t it a short?”

I find it to be. I’m proud that, in the beginning screenings, I wasn’t telling people the runtime. I’d ask them afterward, and they’d say, “It felt like 1:40.”

I went with my 23-year-old club-kid colleague, and it was as if I were watching a foreign film. Edward was translating for me. We also discovered afterward that Edward had received an email asking him to be an extra.

Every gay in New York City did. It’s very much that Madison Beer meme: “I was supposed to be in the video.” Every gay guy in New York.

We had two amazing producers I brought on, Zach Stone and Ben Hubley, who were already in the party scene. I couldn’t have done it without them.

I went to college with Zach Stone, but anyway…

He’s great. He’s the best. They basically sent out mass texts and emails to everyone they’d ever partied with, and people showed up. It was actually a beautiful part of the process, seeing the community show up for creativity. It moved me to see everyone in one space together. They were there to have a good time and party.

I want to know what your relationship with club culture is. I think of you as an LA person, both because of I Love LA and before. Why did you set this in New York club culture rather than LA club culture?

To me, New York club culture has so much history. We’re standing on the backs of giants. From Studio 54 to the ’80s to the actual Club Kids days in the ’90s, it has such a lineage and such a vibe. LA has things going on, but it’s a bit less specific. I also think the idea of marathon partying has changed. I say in the movie, “It’s the Berlinification of New York, mama.” I think that’s happened, where marathon-style partying, starting on Friday and going until Sunday, is a new thing for New York.

Yeah. People really stay out all night. You end up at an afters, then go to another party. I don’t think it’s really meant to be in New York because New York isn’t Berlin. You have to hustle all week. You have to work really hard. I just heard a girl in the Vogue bathroom screaming at someone, “You need to keep your phone on!” So there’s a different vibe. She’s going to watch this and be like, “Oh, shit.”

But I think there’s a different grind in this city. When you put that next to the party scene, it creates a hamster wheel that I think a lot of gay men are living through, loving, but also having problems with. It was interesting for me to set the movie here. Also, New York and a kid are cinematic. There’s a lineage of movies like this that feel cozy, movies I wanted to replicate from the ’70s, like Kramer vs. Kramer.

Gloria, Kramer vs. Kramer, and obviously Big Daddy, which we’ve gotten a lot of comparisons to. There’s also a movie called One Fine Day. Do you remember that?

That was a visual reference. Uptown Girls, obviously. Such a classic. I wanted to be part of that canon, and I felt like a queer retelling of the story was deserved and made sense. We have this delayed coming of age as gay men. We don’t have the same pressures to have kids or settle down, so we keep partying. But eventually that becomes an issue, even for your body. It’s a hard burden to carry, continuing to party every weekend.

When I’ve taken writing classes, they always say that novelists write what they know in their first novel. They write something that feels true. What’s the germ of inspiration for this story? I don’t think you actually have a club kid, but where are you coming from?

It came from a very simple idea. I was at a dinner party with friends who had an eight-year-old son. I was saying inappropriate things in front of him and making him laugh, and the parents were laughing too. I thought there might be something funny in the dynamic between me and a kid.

I feel like a kid in a lot of ways. I think I have a childlike spirit, and I’ve always gotten along with kids. I really like them and relate to them. That was the germ of the idea. Then I had a boyfriend in Berlin, so I was there a lot, partying. It was really fun, but the comedowns were hard, and I think it took away from things.

2021–2024. I basically wrote the first draft before the breakup, and it got really good after the breakup. I’ve been calling it my Adele breakup album.

It is a breakup movie in a lot of ways. There’s a feeling of love in the movie, and I think that comes from the love I had and have for my ex, the times we spent together, and the parties we had together. I see it as a love letter to him. We haven’t spoken since, and I hear he’s doing well. I want him to do well. I felt like I had a lot of love to put on the page, and that’s when it got really good.

I read that you crashed real parties during filming and brought crew members with you. What did that look like? Paint the scene.

I didn’t crash. There were weeks of discussion with the party planners. My friend Noah Shakoor throws a party called Pit Stop, and he was like, “Come shoot the party.” Really, all you have to do is put up a sign saying you’re being filmed. There’s also a very famous afters in New York that goes until 3:00 p.m. the next day. It starts at 7:00 p.m. on Saturday and ends at 3:00 p.m. Sunday.

What do you do for fake bundle or kundle?

There’s actually a funny story here. There’s a scene where I’m supposed to be selling drugs at the party.

For listeners at home, bundle is cocaine.

Bundle is the original. Everything stems from bundle. Kundle is K, gundle is GHB, which I heard you didn’t know about.

You shouldn’t try GHB. I love you, but I don’t think you should try that drug.

I’ve been told I’d be even more insane. People have said I have no chill.

I think molly is good for you. I think you’re a molly girl.

Feel it out. Feel the music. Hug someone. That’s the vibe. Mundle, molly.

Now there are all the numbers—3-MMC, 4-MMC—so thrundle, frundle.

This is too much. You’re supposed to remember this when you’re high?

Everything is undle, so you just add the first initial.

What about ayahuasca? Is there ayawundle?

Oh, wow. I’m going to be using that all week.

I’m imagining the Woody Allen big sneeze scene. Tell me about people doing fake cocaine.

Here’s the story. I was selling fake drugs, and people knew there was a camera there, but they didn’t really know what was happening. They thought something else. They were high. I gave one girl a bump, and she said, “What the fuck? That’s not K.” She started getting really mad at me. I told her it was fake drugs, just powdered lactose. She said, “I’m lactose intolerant.” I actually ended up drugging someone with fake drugs by giving them that.

I had powdered vitamin D or C and powdered lactose. I found that the lactose looked the most like coke. The vitamin C looked more like K. It was a little saltier-looking. There’s a lot of trickery. Diego, our props guy, shout-out. What a fun job for the props department.

We talk a lot at Vogue about the different etiquette for different situations. What is club etiquette?

I think club etiquette is actually very boundaried and respectful. If you lose control at the club, it’s not a good look. Especially in Berlin. When I was new to Berlin, my friend was DJing behind the decks. I was trying to say hi and get his attention, and someone told me no. It’s a no-no to distract the DJ. There are definitely rules. You don’t want to be messy. Having a good attitude is important, too. You don’t want to be a bitch.

Anytime I’m in a group and people have an attitude, I’m like, “What are we doing here?” We’re here to have a good time. Be open to new people. Read the room. If someone doesn’t want to talk to you, move on. Those are my rules at the club.

Those are good rules. You also cast a lot of people who are club habitués. How did you find these individuals? What was it like working with people who aren’t actors? This was also your first directing experience.

I did everything they say you shouldn’t do on your first film. I worked with a kid, worked with non-actors, shot on film, shot at real parties, and shot in New York. There were days when I thought, Why did I do this to myself? But as far as the non-actors go, they just came. We had so much history together by that point. We were friends, and I’d been partying with Saturn for years. I’d also partied with Alaska [Riley].

She always felt like such a maternal figure in the LA party scene, but also had this power and confidence while being warm. I always wanted that for the character. The movie is about the different types of families we can create. Saffron is a mother figure to Arlo in a lot of ways.

She shockingly convinced me, as someone with a four- and six-year-old, that it was a good idea for him to DJ.

That’s the trick of the movie. You see it and you’re like, “Sure, we’re making this argument.” Then the second time, you’re like, “This is a terrible plan.” That was intentional. I wanted you to feel like you’re watching this kid learn and express himself creatively, and you’re like, “Yes, yes, yes.” Then you’re like, “No, no, no.”

It’s interesting to think about because there’s obviously a way to nurture creativity, even in a DJ setting, without actually bringing a kid to the club. There are so many merits to having these queer ideas in a kid’s life, while also being realistic and practical. That’s the whole thing with Peter. He’s doing the baseline of parenting, which is love. He’s also a pushover to everyone, especially Arlo. He just wants Arlo to have everything he wants.

I love him. This kid is such a star. I hear he also teased and bullied you.

I gave him permission. He would say things like, “You’re being really cringe right now,” or, “That’s such a dad joke.” I became the embarrassing dad to him. Now I do things to make him cringe because I enjoy it. I feel like a dad to him sometimes, and it’s fun. I think that’s what made him comfortable on set: being able to make fun of me.

It’s hard to work with a young person in that way, and also to get to know them.

That’s one thing I hope people take away from the movie: having a more honest relationship with kids. It’s something I wish I’d had when I was younger. I didn’t know my parents were people. That never occurred to me. I don’t think they really saw me as a person, either. They saw me as their kid. I wish I’d known more about their struggles or what they were feeling inside. I think it would’ve taught me more about what life was going to be like. We have these barriers between parents and kids that I think we could soften a little.

I want to know whether Reggie is actually into music. I was impressed with how seamless it was.

Yes, majorly. But his music taste has completely changed. He’s at that age where he’s impressionable. He came in loving Slipknot and Metallica. He was like, “I like the hard stuff.” Now he loves Cocteau Twins and Massive Attack. His whole playlist has changed. We have a shared playlist now.

Wow. Did you start that during the movie?

We started it a bit after the movie, but obviously the movie inspired it. It’s really sweet to watch him change through this experience. Watching him start doing press and interviews, he’s become so mature, grown-up, and funny. I feel like a proud parent. I understand why parents cry watching the school play. You’re like, “Oh my God, this is my kid. He’s up there doing it, and he’s so good.”

He’s so good. I hear you guys have been DJing together.

He DJs, I’m the hype man. I’m in the back. I don’t know all the knobs and stuff. Saturn taught him the basics. He knows his highs, lows, and mids.

Jordan Firstman, Reggie Absolom, and Saturn Risin9 at the after-party for the Los Angeles premiere of Club Kid.

Absolutely. I also heard that you brought the straight film crew to Basement and were teaching them some lingo. What was that Duolingo lesson like?

Duolingo, cundle edition. We need to get the Duolingo owl.

That’s an A24 idea. Are you here? That’s a really good idea. Pay her some money. You have a good job; you don’t need the extra funds. I’ll take the Duolingo license.

The question was about bringing your straight crew to Basement and teaching everyone.

They just had the best time. It’s not like they’d never partied before, but exposing them to the exact type of queer people the movie was about was important. I wanted everyone to deeply understand the details. The movie comes alive with the details, even the types of furniture a New York gay guy has in his apartment. We have Butt magazine, and we have our own specific things.

A lot of chrome right now. There’s one kind of chain-link table that a lot of gay guys have. It’s a lot of chrome and black. Black leather couch. Chic, but also a little grimy.

I want to know if you ever considered having someone else play Peter.

Keanu Reeves. No, this was always my goal and my dream. I thought of people like Lena Dunham, Donald Glover, Phoebe Waller-Bridge. I knew I had a voice, and I knew I could act a bit, so it was always the plan.

I’m always impressed with people who can do all three. You wrote it, directed it, and acted in it. How did you move between those three roles?

I tried to think of it like Pandora’s box. The movie was the box, and everything went into it. All my jobs were funneling into the project. I tried not to separate my actor brain from my writer brain or my director brain. If I did that, I’d start to freak out and lose control of the whole thing. Everything was in service of getting all the ideas, vision, and craft into one thing.

That’s such a good question. I think it’s both a mistake I made and something I don’t regret. You can tell in a club scene when people are faking dancing. I had a looped beat and thought we should dance to it to get the energy up. My sound guy said we really needed to do takes without sound because the edit would be a nightmare. The edit was a nightmare because you have to go beat by beat. But the crowd had more energy because the music was playing. It was a double-edged sword: harder in the edit, but better for the movie.

You’ve cast a lot of your friends in your projects. Do you ever find it difficult to work with friends? Is it harder to give direction?

I found that both the party host in me and the people-pleaser in me conflicted with directing. I wanted everyone to have a good time. Part of shooting is doing a lot of takes. It’s not comfortable and it’s annoying. Some of my friends didn’t realize that they’d do a scene full-out, and I’d say, “That’s the wide, and we’re not really going to use the wide.” By the time we got to their coverage, they didn’t have the same energy as they did in the wide.

Especially in those group scenes, where I had every cool gay guy in New York, excluding your friend who didn’t show up to work. I felt bad asking them to do 10 takes of dancing. They were sweaty and tired and they weren’t getting paid that much. That was the hardest part: getting what I needed as a director while keeping everyone happy.

I want to know about your crazy T-shirt collection. That was one of my favorite things. We’ve been talking a lot on the Vogue fashion team about how there’s a real slogan T-shirt renaissance right now.

What’s your favorite slogan T-shirt in the rotation right now?

I have some things I’m making. We made an “Innit Babes” one for the movie. We’re going to make a “Mama, Let’s Research” one.

I saw you made “I Boofed at Cannes and All I Got Was This Lousy Shirt.”

That was a good one. I’m going to go with “I Boofed at Cannes” because people didn’t understand what either of the two T-shirts I made for Cannes meant. There was “I Need a Doll Line and a Sickening Dose Stat” and “I Boofed at Cannes and All I Got Was This Lousy Shirt.” The beauty of them was that no one understood either of them.

I loved your character in I Love LA. You’re a stylist. What did you do to research for that role? Are there stylists you look to?

There’s one stylist whose aesthetic I’d been looking at. I won’t say who it is. In LA, you’re around a lot of creative people. Being friends with Charli, you see her whole glam team. Makeup people and hair people have their own personalities. Stylists have their own personalities.

I’ve been around a lot of stylists, so I was channeling some specific behaviors. But I was also thinking about the loneliness you can have as a gay stylist. You’re basically a paid best friend to these celebrities. You’re there when they need you, but then at a certain point you have to leave and go do more work for them. Sometimes they yell at you. Sometimes you mess up, and sometimes things aren’t perfect. It’s a hard job being a stylist, and you don’t get a lot of gratitude from the world or sometimes even the people you work with.

I Love LA has started production on season two, or not yet?

Ended. It comes out very soon. I don’t think I can say the date, but soon. Sooner than you could ever imagine.

What’s the vibe on set with that crew? Do you have group chats? How much are you guys in touch?

All the time. We’ve become family, and the set is so easy and fun. We improvise, help each other with our looks, and it’s definitely family vibes now.

I want to know what your cultural diet is right now. Have you seen the Elizabeth Holmes documentary?

I have. I was in the room at Telluride when they dropped it.

It was a pure, unadulterated gag. I can’t remember the last time I was squeezing someone’s hand like that. I couldn’t believe it. The “Who’s on First?” thing really got me.

Sorry. Never mind. It was a once-in-a-lifetime kind of thing. It only would have worked with those two people together. There aren’t two other people who could make that movie work. I think it’s a real accomplishment.

Remi Wolf. Her new album is so good. She has a song called “I Won’t Cry” that’s one of the best love songs I’ve ever heard. I’m also getting into the new Troye Sivan album. “Fucking Forever,” another good love song. I’m newly in love, maybe, so I’m into love songs right now.

What’s the dream date when you’re newly in love?

I actually like a party. I love a classic walk. If we’re in Paris, walking, eating, and sitting by the river is always romantic. New York, too. A little rain never hurt anyone. I love a first date with kissing. Sitting at a bar for a long time and just kissing is really hot to me.

For a gay guy, it’s kind of transgressive to only kiss on a first date. It’s sexy not to know what’s going on underneath the pants and just keep it to mouth stuff. I also like going to the club with someone I love, doing a little molly, and making out. That’s really sweet and fun to me.

Okay. Let’s do it. Then I have to run upstairs.

Who is the worst type of person to party with?

Who’s the best type of person to party with?

Strappy. We’ve seen it. Strappy and midriff. We’ve seen it.

I’m such a gay guy. I think a white tank top is hot and looks good. A black tank top, too. Shorts. Athletic shorts are so hot at the club.

3:00 to 4:00 a.m. I think it’s a really cool hour. Things get a little loosey-goosey, but not cracky.

That’s when you want loosey-goosey, not cracky.

Loosey-goosey, not cracky-wacky. You’re not only learning lingo, you’re inventing it. I’m helping gay culture. You’re really pushing the culture forward.

An espresso martini at the club is kind of a vibe.

That doesn’t conflict with the mundle or the trundle?

If you’re doing G, you can’t drink anything. If I’m being real, a crisp Diet Coke. Or a mate. In Berlin, they have Club-Mate. Mate and vodka is really nice.

Favorite post-club snack? This is where our interests can overlap.

You know what? I like a soup. Something nourishing, like a vegetable soup or pho. You want to go fried, but your body actually wants something nourishing.

Let’s go pho. You say to someone, “Let’s go pho. Let’s get out of here and go pho.”

This conversation has been edited and condensed.

‘The Wizard of Oz’ Was a Big Reference for Doc ‘A Want in Her.’ Now Its Creator Is Moving Into Fiction.

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Irish artist-turned-filmmaker Myrid Carten, who won three BIFAs for her feature debut and made the U.K. BAFTA Breakthrough list, talks to THR about her journey so far – and what lies ahead.

About 11 months ago, Myrid Carten’s debut feature film, the documentary A Want in Her, an exploration of intergenerational trauma, mental health and addiction, was honored with three British Independent Film Awards (BIFAs), namely for best feature documentary, the best debut director – feature documentary, and The Raindance Maverick Award.

On Wednesday, the Irish artist-turned-filmmaker was revealed as a member of the 2026 U.K. BAFTA Breakthrough cohort, supported by Netflix.

A Want in Her was written and directed by Carten, who also appears in the doc and handled cinematography. It follows Carten and her mother, Nuala, who goes missing somewhere in Ireland. The creative’s search for her takes her into a family feud, a house dispute and a history that threatens to take everyone down, including the filmmaker herself. The doc had its world premiere at the 2024 International Documentary Film Festival Amsterdam (IDFA) in Amsterdam. Related Stories Movies 'La Bola Negra' Ups Planned Theater Count Heading Into Netflix Film's Widest Weekend Yet (Exclusive) Movies AFM Hot Project: Jacob Tremblay, Kate McKinnon Join 'I'm With Stupid' (Exclusive)

On the occasion of her inclusion on the U.K. BAFTA Breakthrough list, Carten talked to THR about her journey behind A Want in Her, just how vulnerable she made herself in the process and what’s next for her.

What led you to take on such a challenging film project, for which you made yourself so vulnerable?

I was led very much by impulse, which comes from my artistic background. I studied fine art, and there, if you have an idea, follow it. Don’t think too much about outcomes! And then, as time went on and the film started to grow, I developed what I call my north star. I felt this adult-child story, the story of the children of people who suffer from mental illness and addiction, isn’t really told that often. So I felt compelled to do it for that particular cohort.

But even in the development process, everybody I met was like, “Oh, I have experience with that.” There’s a statistic that every addict touches 10 other people’s lives. I felt the addict story is often the one that’s told because it’s dramatic with a capital D, but this story felt really important.

On the vulnerability issue, I just really focused on making a film that worked. With myself as a character, it actually felt in some ways that the vulnerability served the film.

I was never thinking about BAFTA or BIFAs when I was in the middle of this old, derelict house and all this drama. My dream was just that the film would be seen in some cinemas. That was the height of my aspiration, and it’s gone beyond that. It’s done better than I thought it would do, especially because it’s quite an experimental film. So the fact that audiences have really taken to it has been a great boost of confidence. People are very smart. I think we often don’t acknowledge how much audiences get things, even if they’re not spelled out for them.

What is your hope for BAFTA Breakthrough?

BAFTA are the best of British and arguably world cinema. I was nominated for this outstanding debut category, and there tends to be one doc in there. But I really want to make a fiction film.

That’s always been my desire. I tried to use as many dramaturgy elements in A Want in Her as I could. I think it’s going to be an interesting move into fiction and blurring that space.

It is just amazing with that transition into fiction because I feel BAFTA is really a bed for lots of producers, directors, creative talent who are all drawn to BAFTA. So the idea is that I am going to create meetings.

What BAFTA Breakthrough does is it puts you in the role of a mentee, and you can ask people for meetings in more of a mentor capacity as opposed to meetings that are all about collaborating and business. I’m really interested in talking to people who have done debut fiction films that I think are extraordinary and chatting to them about how they did it, not just navigate the industry, but maintain artistic integrity.

I’m also really looking forward to having the BAFTA [View streaming] player and watching all the films. When you have a toddler, you can’t go to the cinema that much. So this is perfect for me.

Do you already have any ideas for a fiction film that you want to start developing?

I have one idea for a doc hybrid that I’ve put on the back shelf, and I’m developing two ideas for fiction films. I am trying to think about how to bring that sense of reality and rawness to this form that is artificial or has artifice in it. Doing screenplays is a whole new process. My story editor always says you learn about making a film by making a film.

Is there anything else you’d like to share?

I’d love to have a career like Jonathan Glazer, in that each of his films is so different. But the person I’d really love to meet through BAFTA Breakthrough would be someone like Lynne Ramsay.

But I have my own path, and actually, that’s what really worked in A Want in Her. I wasn’t trying to make a film like anybody else. I have to really hold onto that.

And the big reference for A Want in Her was The Wizard of Oz, which most people wouldn’t even know. Wait, what!? Now I am curious to hear more…

At the start, we called it No Place Like Home. That was the working title for years, and we would shoot the house as though it was Technicolor. It was this idea of this dream, this fantasy of the house and trying to return to that. So, [The Wizard of Oz] was one of the early references, but it’s so buried that people wouldn’t even recognize that. I like when things are kind of subliminal.

1919 Investment Counsel Expands Senior Client Advisory Team

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BIRMINGHAM, Ala., Nov. 5, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- 1919 Investment Counsel, LLC ("1919"), a subsidiary of Stifel Financial Corp. ("Stifel"), is pleased to announce the appointment of James Russell Sugg, Jr. as Principal, Senior Client Advisor. A licensed North Carolina attorney, Russell brings extensive experience across the legal, trust, and wealth management fields, with particular expertise in trusts and investment management. After 17 years practicing law, he transitioned into wealth management, serving as a Senior Trust Advisor at Wachovia Trust and later at Sterne Agee. His multidisciplinary background brings a valuable perspective to helping clients navigate complex financial needs and pursue their long-term goals.

"Russell brings a distinctive combination of legal, trust, and investment management experience that will be a tremendous asset to our clients and our team," said Hanson Slaughter, Managing Director and Head of the Birmingham office of 1919. "Just as importantly, his thoughtful, relationship-driven approach aligns closely with how we serve clients at 1919. We are very pleased to welcome him to the firm."

In this role, Russell will work with individuals, families, foundations, and endowments, delivering comprehensive investment counsel. He will focus on understanding each client's unique goals in order to develop and implement tailored solutions to meet their long-term wealth and investment objectives. Russell is based in North Carolina and is a member of the Birmingham team.

"I'm thrilled to join 1919 and become part of a firm with such a strong commitment to personalized client service," said Russell Sugg. "My legal background allows me to approach each client's financial and planning needs from a unique perspective, taking into account not only how to build and preserve wealth, but also how to ultimately pass it on to the people and causes that matter most to them."

As of September 30, 2026, 1919 manages approximately $27.4 billion in assets, including $3.2 billion in responsible investing strategies. The firm serves a diverse client base, including individuals and families, corporations, foundations, charitable trusts, and educational and other nonprofit institutions. 1919 has built a reputation for delivering a high-touch, personalized client experience since its founding in 1919. The firm has been recognized as one of the nation's top registered investment advisory firms by Barron's, Financial Advisor Magazine, and CNBC.

About 1919 Investment Counsel: 1919 Investment Counsel ("1919") is a registered investment advisor. Its mission for more than 100 years has been to provide investment counsel and insight that helps families, individuals, foundations, and endowments achieve their financial goals. The firm is headquartered in Baltimore and has offices across the country in Birmingham, Cincinnati, New York, Philadelphia, San Francisco, and Vero Beach. For more information on 1919's services, please visit https://1919ic.com/.

About Stifel: Stifel Financial Corp. (NYSE: SF) is a financial services holding company headquartered in St. Louis, Missouri, that conducts its banking, securities, and financial services business through several wholly owned subsidiaries. Stifel's broker-dealer clients are served in the United States through Stifel, Nicolaus & Company, Incorporated, including its Eaton Partners and Miller Buckfire business divisions; Keefe, Bruyette & Woods, Inc.; and Stifel Independent Advisors, LLC; in Canada through Stifel Nicolaus Canada Inc.; and in the United Kingdom and Europe through Stifel Nicolaus Europe Limited. The Company's broker-dealer affiliates provide securities brokerage, investment banking, trading, investment advisory, and related financial services to individual investors, professional money managers, businesses, and municipalities. Stifel Bank and Stifel Bank & Trust offer a full range of consumer and commercial lending solutions. Stifel Trust Company, N.A. and Stifel Trust Company Delaware, N.A. offer trust and related services. To learn more about Stifel, please visit the Company's website at www.stifel.com. For global disclosures, please visit https://www.stifel.com/investor-relations/press-releases.

Christian Horner Doesn’t Sound Like He’s Done With Formula 1 Yet

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

“I feel like I’m too young to retire,” he says.

“How would you introduce yourself to somebody that doesn’t know you?” I ask the man across the table.

“Christian Horner,” he says, then without pause, “somebody that used to be involved in Formula 1.”

Considering this is a man who could have truthfully responded “Commander of the Order of the British Empire” or “husband of Ginger Spice,” the choice to define himself by his past rather than present or future is hard to miss. His is a familiar name and a familiar face to even casual F1 fans. The Brit’s sharp tongue and vaguely Daniel Craig-ish visage made him a lead character of Netflix’s Drive to Survive in its first years when his Red Bull Formula 1 team was banking win after win, thanks also to the talents of people like Max Verstappen and Adrian Newey.

But no hot streak lasts forever, no matter how good you (or the people you work with) are. Horner was ejected from the team after the 2025 British Grand Prix in the wake of both a sexual harassment accusation scandal the year before and a general downturn in the team’s performance, reportedly receiving a €60 million platinum parachute in exchange for his departure from the sport.

For most, that might seem a sign from the universe to take up a hobby—say, gentleman farmer. But Horner isn’t most men. He’s still out there, orbiting F1 like a geosynchronous satellite: a long ways off but still locked above it, looking down at the whole of the world he left. As such, he’s been speaking at industry events like Sportico Invest London and the Autosport Business Exchange in New York, where I sat down with him before he went on stage.

Unsurprisingly to anyone who saw him on Drive to Survive, or at any of the 10,000 media events he did during his F1 career, Horner still has plenty to say. Even if he is trying to be polite about it.

“The racing has been entertaining, don’t get me wrong. I’m watching these races and seeing cars overtaking in Melbourne and other circuits that you don’t normally overtake at,” he says when discussing the current F1 season. “But they’re not really overtakes as opposed to ... drive-pasts.”

He is not, it seems, a particular fan of the 2026 power unit regulations that have caused consternation amongst teams and fans alike by forcing the gas-electric hybrid propulsion systems to split their output 50/50 between internal combustion-sourced and electron-derived power. Horner describes it as the biggest issue for F1 to tackle at the moment. “You would assume it would be a relatively easy fix, just to change that ratio, but it seems a little more complicated,” he says. “You hear the drivers not liking how these cars are to drive, because you’re not rewarded for driving quickly through a fast corner, for example.”

“I think they’re doing the right thing for 2030,” he adds, referring to FIA president Mohammed Ben ‌Sulayem’s plans to ditch the current hybrid V-6 powertrains for V-8 units. “I think it’s unfortunate for the current presidency because he inherited this set of regulations. So of course, he’s looking to address that for the next round of significant change to engine spec.”

The 2026 season’s power unit frustrations haven’t been all bad, at least in terms of entertainment value. They’ve played a role in keeping the constructors’ championship battle interesting, even if the driver’s championship was swept away by wunderkind Kimi Antonelli.

“It’s interesting, I think different teams have had different moments this year,” he says. “Mercedes started very strong; Ferrari then had a moment where they looked like they were gonna put a challenge together around Barcelona time—seeing Lewis [Hamilton] win his first race, Charles [Leclerc] winning at Silverstone. And McLaren started to get their act together, looked pretty racy around Zanvoort and Budapest time, and then obviously, the Red Bull cars have come on strong.”

But those cars at front of the field are practically in a different league from the other seven teams in this season, which Horner—despite his own successes captaining Red Bull teams that ran the table with Brady-Belichick efficiency—says isn’t ideal for the sport. “I think you want as many cars in the fight as you can get, and the best way to achieve that is stability. Because then they all [close on] the same solutions, and just iterate those same solutions. So if you want close racing, just leave those regulations, those chassis regulations, alone. And you’ll see them all converge onto one solution—the better solution.”

In other words, simplify, then add budget. Formula 1, as he points out, is both one of the most expensive sports in the world and the biggest team sport on the planet. “You’ve got over 2000 team members between engine and chassis, all producing two cars for twenty-four races a year.” Running all as team principal, unsurprisingly, is like spinning plates and juggling bowling pins at the same time, even before factoring in the spotlight.

“Challenges ... there are many. There’s the scrutiny you come under. The politics, the competition of people trying to nick your staff, your drivers, your sponsors. It’s such a competitive industry. There’s so much at stake with large amounts of money involved” he says. “It’s dog-eat-dog in that respect. You gotta get up pretty early in the morning to stay ahead of the rest.”

Having done so for so many years—and most of those seeing an above-average amount of success for the position—certainly suggests the 52-year-old Brit has what Liam Neeson might describe as a particular set of skills. Would you, I ask him, eventually like to circle back to Formula 1?

“I think ...” Here, he pauses. “You know, I had an incredible run. I’m very proud of what I did. It didn’t end the way in the way I would have liked. I’m still relatively young ... I would only go back—knowing the sacrifices it takes—for the right project. Only if I was in a position where I could influence change, drive change within the organization, with like-minded people that wanted to win ... I’ve won close to one-third of the races I entered. I don’t want to go back just to take part.”

But he’s not pigeonholing himself. “I have time on my side to be able to make decisions. I feel like I’m too young to retire,” he says. “Honestly, sitting right here, right now, I have no idea where the next twelve months are going to take me. And I find that kind of exciting.”

Will Sabel Courtney is a contributing editor at Road & Track. A 12-year veteran of journalism, he formerly worked at Gear Patrol, The Drive, and RIDES Magazine, and contributed to the New York Daily News and Jalopnik, among other outlets. You can often find him test-driving new cars in New York City, cursing the slow-moving traffic surrounding him.

Saudi-Backed LIV Golf, After Five Years of Trying to Challenge PGA Tour, Is Shutting Down

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

LIV Golf, the pro-golfing startup venture whose biggest investor had been the sovereign wealth fund of Saudi Arabia, is shutting down after it couldn’t reach an agreement with a new investor on a bankruptcy reorganization.

The league was founded in 2021 by a group of pro golfers, led by Phil Mickleson and Greg Norman, who were dissatisfied with the sport’s biggest player, the PGA Tour. Saudi Arabia’s Public Investment Fund, which was LIV Golf’s primary backer, invested more than $5 billion into the venture — before the PIF in April 2026 said it was ending its funding. Related Stories NewsNation Plans Fast-Paced Election Night Focused on Hot Data, Not Hot Air MS NOW Has 2026 Midterm Elections Coverage: Here's How to Watch Election Night Results Live Online Free

After the Saudis exited, LIV Golf had proposed a recapitalization transaction with BC Partners Advisors through its voluntary Chapter 11 bankruptcy proceedings in the U.S. Bankruptcy Court for the District of New Jersey. However, according to a statement from the league, “the relevant parties were ultimately unable to reach a consensual resolution of certain outstanding terms of the transaction.”

As such, LIV Golf announced that it intends to liquidate its assets and wind down the business, but added that “the company will remain open to, and continue to consider, any value-maximizing alternatives with third parties that may arise.”

BC Partners Credit had proposed investing $300 million into LIV Golf but deal talks fell apart. In a statement, the financial firm said, “BC Partners Credit supported a transaction that aligned the interests of players and investors, offering a credible path toward building a sustainable future for LIV Golf. While there was great support for the league, we are disappointed that mutually acceptable terms could not be reached among the various stakeholders.”

In January 2025, Fox Sports and LIV Golf announced a multiyear media rights agreement for live coverage of the league’s competition in the U.S. Under the pact, Fox carried live coverage LIV Golf League’s 14-tournament global schedule with events in the U.S., Asia, Australia, Europe and Latin America; LIV Golf tournaments had previously aired on Nexstar Media Group’s CW network. A rep for Fox Sports did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The top-ranked golfers in the LIV Golf League this season included Jon Rahm, Bryson DeChambeau, Joaquin Niemann, Lucas Herbert, Tyrrell Hatton, Thomas Detry, Anthony Kim and Sergio Garcia.

LIV Golf CEO Scott O’Neil said in a statement Thursday, “Building a global platform designed to bring new, younger, and more diverse fans to the sport of golf has been the round of a lifetime for all of us. LIV Golf was fun, demanding, inspiring, at times challenging — but always rewarding. … We created memories that I hope will inspire a new generation of fans to follow, play, and fall in love with this great game.”

In LIV Golf’s liquidation, Gibson, Dunn & Crutcher is serving as lead legal counsel, Cole Schotz is serving as co-legal counsel, David Orlofsky of AlixPartners is serving as chief restructuring officer, Ducera Partners is serving as investment banker, and C Street Advisory Group is serving as strategic communications adviser.

French authorities investigate claim of fraud over Picasso plates and medallions on sale

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pablo Picasso with a silver plate, in 1957. The artist had commissioned François Hugo to produce repoussé metal pieces from his designs © 2026 Succession Picasso/DACS, London; © Photo: Edward Quinn, © edwardquinn.com

A criminal investigation amid a family crisis casts a dark shadow over the illustrious history of the French studio Ateliers Hugo, which produced jewellery and silverware created by major artists of the 20th century. According to a legal source, the court of Aix-en-Provence in the south of France has opened an investigation following a complaint by the Picasso family.

On 10 March, Paloma Ruiz-Picasso, representing the nine heirs of the Spanish artist, filed a complaint alleging “fraud, counterfeiting, forgery and concealment of these felonies”. The file does not name the alleged perpetrators, and their identity is not presently known. The following month the Picasso family published a formal warning in the Gazette Drouot, titled “Hugo silverware”. It urged “all art market professionals and buyers to exercise the utmost vigilance”, following the appearance of what it described as “a large number” of illegitimate silver plates and gold medallions, valued at millions of euros.

Paloma Ruiz-Picasso, the daughter of Pablo, has filed a legal complaint on behalf of nine family members of the artist about the allegedly unauthorised works © Studio Cabrelli

The title refers to Ateliers Hugo, a goldsmith based in Aix-en-Provence. It was founded in 1933 by François Hugo (1899-1981), the great-grandson of the famous French author Victor Hugo. In 1955 Pablo Picasso, who since 1947 had created a variety of ceramics in Vallauris on the French Riviera, commissioned François Hugo to produce plates and dishes from similar designs in fine metals, using the traditional technique of repoussé. Later, the workshop reduced them into medallions and earrings. With the help of his brother, the painter Jean Hugo, who introduced him to artistic circles, François made jewellery designed by Salvador Dalí, Max Ernst, André Derain, Jean Arp, Jean Dubuffet, Roberto Matta, Jean Lurçat and Dorothea Tanning. In 1975, his son Pierre Hugo (1947-2023) took over the company. Pierre was followed by his son Nicolas (born 1989).

Members of the Picasso family and their lawyer, Jean-Jacques Neuer, who filed the complaint, declined to comment to The Art Newspaper. But their complaint alleges that “it has been discovered that the moulds of Picasso’s works were reused for unauthorised productions of silver and gold pieces, followed by duplicates, some of them bearing three times the same number, and unnumbered specimens”.

According to a source close to the investigation, the Picasso Administration, which represents the family, says it has discovered at least 17 complete or almost complete suspicious collections, amounting to hundreds of pieces. The first red flag was raised by the Museum of Old and New Art (Mona), a private Australian museum in Tasmania. According to this source, in May 2023 the museum informed the Picasso Administration of its doubts over plates it had bought at Sotheby’s in London. An auction catalogue of 13 October 2021 describes a “complete set of 24 silver plates” bearing the signature of the artist, completed with an additional “unnumbered proof of Visage aux mains”. The pieces were “accompanied by a certificate of authenticity issued by Pierre Hugo”. The lot was sold for £1.4m. Sotheby’s declined to comment for this story.

According to the legal files, the seller was the British dealer Mark Maurice, from a now dissolved gallery named Masterpiece. The source close to the investigation says the dealer allegedly produced documents showing that he had commissioned a set of plates and a set of medallions from the Ateliers Hugo between 2017 and 2020. Mark Maurice indicated he was “unable to respond” to The Art Newspaper’s inquiries “due to strict non-disclosure agreements in place”.

In summer 2023 the ceramics museum in Vallauris, Musée Magnelli, exhibited another collection of 24 silver plates as well as gold medallions attributed to Picasso.

In 2023, the Musée Magnelli in Vallauris exhibited plates and medallions attributed to Picasso Travelworld

According to the legal files, the Picasso Administration protested, having found that they bore no number, mark or punch, as legally required. Nicolas Hugo acknowledges these facts, but tells The Art Newspaper that the works exhibited at Musée Magnelli were so-called “author’s copies, made for the exhibition, duly indicating in the catalogue that they were not for sale”. He adds that he “recovered them and had them melted after the show”. Representatives for the museum did not respond to a request for comment.

In June 2023, as reported in the legal files, the French branch of the auction house Bonhams made a request to the Picasso Administration for the right to reproduce silverware for a catalogue. The auction house was planning a sale of 95 lots from the Ateliers Hugo collection, including works by Ernst, Derain and Cocteau, valued between €400 and €50,000 each. The most valuable were 59 pieces, “made by” or “after” Picasso.

On 26 September 2023 Bonhams was informed by the Picasso Administration that these pieces were considered to be “unauthorised” editions. The auction house then decided to “delay the whole sale”, just two days before it was due to take place. Nicolas Hugo acknowledges he “did initiate, in the wake of good results obtained at Bonhams UK, this sale of pieces”, produced, he says, by his “father and grandfather”. But, he adds, “When we were informed by dealers of the presence of possible duplicates, I agreed to withdraw the sale and collaborated with the Picasso Administration to identify them.”

Bonhams declined to comment, but according to the source close to the investigation, the lots attributed to Picasso were sequestered when a British financial company, Suros Capital, tried to recover them as collateral for a €256,000 loan. A spokesperson for Suros confirmed the operation to The Art Newspaper, adding that “it was secured against not only the Picasso pieces, but a range of assets which are progressively realised, so the company expects to recover the loan in full”.

In November 2023, according to the source close to the investigation, a representative for Bonhams France informed the Picasso Administration that a Spanish collector contacted the auction house to sell 24 silver plates, estimated at £900,000. Again, the auction house refused on the basis of Picasso Administration’s doubts about the objects’ authenticity. In the legal files, the Picasso Administration claims the same collector had sold another set of 24 silver plates and one of 24 gold medallions in private sales through Bonhams in London in 2022. Neither the British auction house nor the Spanish collector made any comment.

Nicolas Hugo, who says he has not been interviewed by the investigators, claims he has no knowledge of the proceedings and “is unaware of the content of the complaint” issued by the Picasso Administration. But, he says: “The criminal investigation can only deal with facts related to the time of my father and grandfather, therefore falling under the statute of limitations and concerning persons now deceased.” He adds that “the rights to reproduce the items disappeared with them, so I have never minted any of Picasso’s creations”, and he does not have the legal status of a goldsmith. However, he says: “I have signed some certificates of authenticity, but only to confirm my father’s own signature.”

In interviews with The Art Newspaper, Nicolas Hugo describes himself as a “self-taught art dealer”. When he was around 20 years old, he started to work as an assistant in galleries in Paris, before moving to London. He is now an agent for several artists and a curator. In 2022, the French magazine Les Hardis described him as a “mix of British and Saint-Germain-des-Prés charm”. “My father became very ill in 2018,” he told the magazine. “I had an epiphany. I decided to take over the workshop. I immersed myself completely,” and started to train in manufacturing jewels.

“From 2019,” he tells The Art Newspaper, “I became involved in the history of his workshop and helped my father to showcase this heritage, for memorial and commercial reasons. I set up exhibitions in places like Barcelona or Vallauris and initiated this sale of the fonds d’atelier at Bonhams Paris. I have not taken over my father’s company and was not his partner.”

Nicolas Hugo says: “There have been some duplicates on the market, dating from my father’s period, but I have no idea who had made them in the workshop.” He says he has “no idea who put a collection on sale at Sotheby’s in 2021” and knows “nothing of the three sets proposed by this Spanish collector to Bonhams in 2022 and 2023”.

The criminal investigation is still at the preliminary phase. It comes at a time when the company is already in limbo. Pierre Hugo died in October 2023 and his five children have turned down his inheritance. Nicolas Hugo’s brothers and sisters declined to comment to The Art Newspaper due to the ongoing investigation.

However, sources close to the case say that Pierre Hugo’s heirs’ inheritance came with a €2.5m tax debt. According to these sources, three of the Hugo siblings said in police interviews they were “not at all” aware of the conduct of the workshop by their brother Nicolas, who had succeeded their ailing father in 2019. The sources said the three Hugo siblings were on alert after learning that Bonhams Paris declined to sell the collection from the Ateliers Hugo in September 2023. According to the same sources, when Pierre Hugo died a month later, the three siblings went to the workshop and took 21 moulds of plates that they decided to hand over to the Picasso Administration. Three moulds from the set were missing and they did not find those for the medallions.

According to these sources, the three Hugo siblings explained to the investigators that, “knowing that the rights of reproduction were extinct, they wanted to be sure this would be respected”. The siblings also asserted that they “did not want in any way to be implicated in these operations and made it clear to the Picasso Administration”, according to the sources.

Together with his wife Monique Mueller, the Swiss collector built one of the largest private holdings of Pre-Columbian, African and Oceanic objects in Europe

Japanese enjoy art more than French, says curator of Lille Métropole Museum of Modern Art

The third-generation Ateliers Hugo has been casting artists’ creations using ancient techniques from the same workshop in the south of France for nearly 70 years

Wyndham Is Giving Members Free Hotel Status Upgrades—Some Can Claim 3,000 Points Instead

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

You have to register, and the gift depends on your current status, where you live, and whether you’re already enrolled in Wyndham’s Insider subscription.

I don’t get excited about Wyndham status, but free status and free points are good things. If you don’t have hotel status today, having something could be an avenue to match somewhere else. So it could be worth a couple of clicks, even more so if you have a Wyndham stay coming up.

Claim the status upgrade by December 30, 2026. It lasts through December 31, 2026, and Wyndham says to allow 24–48 hours for it to take effect. (Do it on December 30 and it may activate and expire simultaneously – yay!) New members can join and claim Gold. Note that you can’t use the promotional status to match to Caesars Rewards.

The upgraded earning rate is determined by your status at checkout. A stay ending January 1 won’t earn the holiday status bonus.

Wyndham normally requires 5 qualifying nights for Gold, 15 for Platinum, and 40 for Diamond. The benefits are modest:

Preferred room choice means a better location or view within the room category you booked. Early check-in and late checkout are subject to availability, generally up to two hours before or after the hotel’s stated time. Diamond suite upgrades depend on availability at check-in.

As I explained when I looked at Wyndham’s instructions to hotels, you should ask for the preferred room and checkout benefits. And there’s no program-wide elite breakfast or lounge benefit. At a hotel earning the usual 10 base points per dollar, moving from Platinum to Diamond adds just 50 points on $100 of eligible spending. That’s 35 cents at a valuation of 0.7 cents per point.

Eligible Diamonds need to claim their 3,000 points by December 31, 2026.

Members who become Diamond through this holiday upgrade don’t qualify for the points gift. Neither do members with a competitor status match unless they’ve completed the required 10 qualifying nights within 90 days of receiving that matched status.

Wyndham’s messaging on this says 3,000 points are enough for a discounted night at any participating hotel. That doesn’t actually match their current award chart.

Following the September 15 devaluation, standard free nights cost 5,000, 15,000, 30,000 or 45,000 points per bedroom. The points plus cash price at a 45,000-point hotel starts at 4,500 points plus the cash portion.

Ultimately Wyndham has lost much of what made it interesting to me: Vacasa redemptions are gone, the easy credit card route to Caesars Diamond ended, and the top award price rose 50%.

Wyndham Rewards Insider normally costs $95 per year and automatically renews. It offers hotel discounts, extra points, and access to deals on flights, cruises, rental cars, activities and entertainment, along with a concierge.

The advertised 50% increase in earning applies to the elite bonus. A Diamond’s bonus rises from 20% to 30%, so ordinary earning at a 10-point-per-dollar hotel goes from 12 to 13 points per dollar or 8.3% more points. And the hotel discount isn’t a promise of the lowest price. Wyndham acknowledges that another rate available to non-Insiders can be cheaper. Buying Platinum could make sense for someone who isn’t, can’t status match, but still plans a bunch of Wyndham stays upcoming.

The holiday upgrade can be combined with Wyndham’s current 4X points promotion. Register before booking, book direct by January 15, 2027, and complete eligible paid stays by January 19. The holiday status itself ends December 31. The multiplier applies to base points, with a cap of 15,000 promotional bonus points. At the normal 10-point base rate, a non-Insider Diamond earns 40 points per dollar from the base earning and promotion, plus 2 elite bonus points (not four times their usual 12). Insiders can earn another 5,000 points after two qualifying nights, either together or across two stays. That bonus doesn’t stack with the separate Wyndham cardholder bonus. Premier cardholders can earn 10,000 instead. For an upcoming stay, claim your status first and register for the points promotion before making the reservation since a booking made before promotion registration doesn’t qualify. Tesla wants its sworn Cybercab answers to NHTSA kept entirely secret

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tesla has answered NHTSA’s Special Order demanding it prove, under oath, that the Cybercab meets federal safety standards. The response landed on the October 30 deadline, and Tesla asked the agency to keep all of it confidential.

The only part of the filing the public can read is a 30-page guide for firefighters. Everything about how a car with no steering wheel, pedals, or mirrors was certified as compliant is blacked out.

We reported in September that NHTSA had ordered Tesla to show its work on the Cybercab’s certification. The order came one week after the company started charging for Cybercab rides in Austin on September 3 and put 21 questions to the company, with civil penalties of up to $139 million for an incomplete answer and criminal exposure for a false one.

Tesla first tried to buy time. On September 18, it asked for eight more weeks, until November 20, citing “the voluminous nature of the request” and the work of searching its records and writing “thorough narrative formats in response to each question.” NHTSA gave it 30 days instead. The new deadline was October 30, and Tesla met it.

Three Tesla documents now sit in the AQ26002 docket, and two of them are empty.

The first is Tesla’s response letter, dated October 30 and addressed from 1 Tesla Road in Austin. It is one page long. The only text is: “The entirety of this file is confidential business information.”

The second is a placeholder for Tesla’s answer to Request 8, which asked the company to explain, standard by standard, how it determined the Cybercab complies: which subpart it certified to, and whether it ran physical tests, simulations, or engineering analyses. Per NHTSA’s note, the responsive documents carry a confidentiality request “for the entirety of each document.”

The third is the only substantive document: Tesla’s Cybercab First Responder Interaction Plan, version 1.2, dated September 3. It’s a real document with useful information in it. It’s also something Tesla has had on its website since July, aimed at police and fire crews, not regulators.

So the public gets a tow-truck manual, and the certification argument stays sealed.

The Special Order wasn’t a fishing expedition. It named the rules the Cybercab doesn’t obviously meet, and it seeks to understand how Tesla believes it can get around it.

FMVSS 135 says “the service brakes shall be activated by means of a foot control.” NHTSA has stated in public that a manufacturer of a vehicle with no foot brake can’t certify to it, then asked Tesla how it did. FMVSS 108 requires a turn-signal control that cancels itself when the steering wheel turns. FMVSS 111 requires mirrors. And the agency wanted to know whether Tesla certified the car with temporary driver controls bolted in and then removed them, which would run into the Safety Act’s “make inoperative” prohibition.

It also asked how the Cybercab is legal without a Part 555 exemption, the route Amazon’s Zoox took for its own steering-wheel-less robotaxi in July. Zoox applied, made its case, and got an exemption capped at 2,500 vehicles a year. Tesla self-certified and started charging for rides.

Every one of those answers is now under a confidentiality claim.

The first-responder plan was written for a different audience, but it brushes against several of NHTSA’s questions.

It says the Cybercab is “not typically equipped with a steering wheel or acceleration and brake pedals.” The word “typically” is doing work there. The same document notes that a Cybercab with a steering wheel and pedals “is typically an engineering or test vehicle, and operates at SAE Level 2 autonomy,” and includes instructions for driving one. That’s relevant to Requests 5 through 7, which asked whether the subject vehicles can be driven with temporarily attached controls.

It describes an operational design domain covering “all public roadways, such as freeways, highways, city streets, and rural roads,” at all hours, in light and moderate rain, fog, and snow. That is a far wider envelope than the Austin geofence the cars run in today, and it references an “Arizona Areas of Operation Appendix.” Request 1 asked for the ODD and Request 2 for scaling plans.

And it confirms that first responders can be granted access to on-board touchscreen controls to move the car, after showing a badge to the B-pillar camera (that’s interesting). Request 20 asked whether the touchscreen can move the vehicle and under what restrictions.

Nothing in it touches brakes, mirrors, telltales, or the certification itself.

Confidentiality requests go to NHTSA’s Chief Counsel under Part 512. The agency decides whether Tesla’s claim holds, and it can order a public version released if it doesn’t. NHTSA hasn’t said anything about the request, and it hasn’t found the Cybercab noncompliant with any standard.

This is familiar. In June 2025, Tesla asked NHTSA to hide its answers about the original Robotaxi launch in Austin, a service running on supervised FSD while FSD was under investigation. Those answers never surfaced.

NHTSA’s opening resume for the audit put the affected population at about 1,000 vehicles. The Cybercab is still carrying passengers in Austin while the agency reads a filing nobody else can.

Tesla’s position in September was that the Cybercab complies with every applicable federal standard, including the one that requires a brake pedal. If that’s true, the argument for it is the most interesting document in the autonomous vehicle industry right now, and Tesla just asked the government to make sure nobody reads it.

We get why a company redacts test data and supplier details. But the legal theory for how a car with no foot brake meets a foot-brake rule doesn’t sound like a trade secret to me. Zoox’s exemption petition is public. Waymo’s safety framework is public. Tesla’s case for why it didn’t need to ask is sealed, cover letter included.

NHTSA now has to decide whether “the entirety of this file” is really confidential business information. If it says no, we’ll finally see how Tesla certified this car. If it says yes, about 1,000 Cybercabs keep driving on a certification the public isn’t allowed to check.

A robotaxi that only runs when the grid is up is a reminder that electricity is the one fuel cost you can actually control at home. California’s Potential Billionaire Tax Has Already Cost The State

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

California’s latest proposed tax increase has already led to so much wealth fleeing the state that tax revenue is set to fall, not rise. Golden State politicians announced the upcoming opening day of hunting season on billionaires and are about to find out they can’t fund welfare programs by taxing people who have already left.

At issue is California’s Proposition 40, a one-time 5% tax on billionaires’ net worth, which proponents claim will raise $100 billion for state-funded health care. Unfortunately for them, the wealthy were paying attention. At least seven high-profile billionaires left the state before Jan. 1 of this year, the residency date that determines who owes the tax.

They took with them roughly $539 billion of the state’s $1.9 trillion in billionaire wealth. Google co-founders Larry Page and Sergey Brin alone shrank the expected haul by $25 billion.

The fact is, $100 billion was never a realistic revenue projection. That estimate ignored losses from departures and legal challenges and both are certain to keep coming. The retroactive residency date alone invites a raft of constitutional challenges.

Supreme Court precedent bars retroactively imposing a “wholly new tax.” Locking people into paying whether they stay or leave, before voters have even approved the tax, burdens the constitutional right to travel. And as the Tax Foundation notes, uncapped penalties on appraisers of hard-to-value assets raise Excessive Fines Clause concerns, since they’re grossly disproportionate to good-faith errors.

>>> California Risks Killing the Golden Goose With Proposition 40

Once departures and litigation are counted, Stanford economists Joshua Rauh and Benjamin Jaros estimate the tax would bring in closer to $30 billion. That’s less than one-third of what was promised, which is bad news for Medi-Cal, the state’s Medicaid program.

But the bigger problem has already arrived. While a wealth tax is collected once, an income tax is collected every year. Rauh and Jaros estimate California’s billionaires pay between $3.3 billion and $5.8 billion in state income tax annually, and every billionaire who relocates takes his share with him permanently.

Weigh that lost income tax revenue against Prop 40’s one-time haul, and the net present value of the tax comes out to negative $38.9 billion.

In other words, the tax is a money-loser for the state. And this estimate isn’t a mere outlier from unlikely assumptions: economists ran 100,000 simulations with different revenue estimates and discount rates, and the state came out behind in 85% of them.

California already depends on a sliver of total earners for most of its tax receipts, with the top 1 percent of earners supplying roughly half of all personal income tax revenue. These are the people creating business, funding startups, and employing thousands of Californians. Losing even a handful of them does real damage to state and family finances alike.

>>> New York’s Property Tax Hike Chases Wealthy Wallets Away

Ironically, the billionaires who flee suffer less than the average Californian. As F. Scott Fitzgerald said, the rich are different from you and me; they can leave for Florida, Tennessee, Texas, or another low-tax state. It’s California’s public services, funded by their income, that will suffer.

Medi-Cal and the other programs counting on this money will still need funding. When $100 billion becomes $30 billion, followed by a permanent reduction to income tax revenue, politicians will clamor for more tax hikes.

But with a shrinking tax base of billionaires, the hikes will inevitably fall on the middle class. A tax sold as making billionaires “pay their fair share” will end up costing the residents not wealthy enough to leave.

Supporters will point to research showing high-earners rarely move because of taxes. But the best-known study examined ordinary income tax rates over many years. A levy on someone’s entire net worth is a different animal and will prompt more people to leave, as seven just did.

Before Californians vote on Prop 40, they should understand how much the tax will realistically raise—and who will get stuck with the actual bill in the long run.
