Christopher Nolan’s ‘The Odyssey’ Will Return to Theaters Next Month as Awards Season Heats Up

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Universal's epic has collected $626 million domestically for a global cume of $1.8 billion.

The theatrical journey continues for Christopher Nolan‘s The Odyssey.

Universal Pictures‘ acclaimed and starry mythological epic is set for a nationwide theatrical rerelease on Dec. 11 after its record-setting run over the summer. Starring Matt Damon as Odysseus, The Odyssey will return to more than 1,000 North American theaters in all formats, including Imax and Imax 70mm.

With Nolan and Emma Thomas producing for Syncopy, The Odyssey has collected $626 million domestically for a global cume of $1.8 billion. It continues to play in international markets in all formats and recently surpassed 2015’s Furious 7 as Universal’s highest-grossing international release ever. Related Stories Movies Robert Pattinson to Be Honored With American Cinematheque Award Business Imax Posts Record Revenue, Profit Growth With 'The Odyssey' in Third Quarter

Universal announced in September that the film had surpassed 2015’s Jurassic World to become the studio’s highest-grossing release of all time. It had previously supplanted 2012’s The Dark Knight Rises as the top-grossing feature in the acclaimed filmmaker’s career after opening on July 17 with Nolan’s biggest debut frame internationally and globally.

This rerelease is well-timed as awards season heats up, with The Odyssey expected to be among the top candidates for an array of Academy Award categories, including best picture. Tom Holland, Anne Hathaway, Robert Pattinson, Lupita Nyong’o, Zendaya and Charlize Theron round out the cast for the feature adaptation of Homer’s ancient poem about Odysseus (Damon) making his way home to Ithaca after the Trojan War.

After becoming the first narrative feature to be shot entirely on Imax film cameras, The Odyssey notched the biggest debut frame domestically for Imax 70mm and delivered the studio’s highest-grossing opening for an R-rated title. Nolan’s film continued to hit milestones throughout its run, becoming the highest-grossing R-rated movie ever, in addition to Imax’s highest-grossing feature ever.

The Odyssey stands just outside the all-time global top 10 chart, holding down the No. 11 spot behind Zootopia 2 and its $1.87 billion. Nolan’s film is No. 15 on the all-time domestic chart and remains below Jurassic World’s $652 million for Universal’s biggest North American haul.

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Swansea City have hit a poor run of form after a phenomenal start to the season. The Swans were top of the pile after 12 games, but a run of three defeats in four games has seen them drop to 4th, with a midweek defeat to Portsmouth the latest in that run.

During that Pompey clash, summer signing Stephen Welsh went off with a knee problem that appeared serious. However, as they head to Birmingham City this weekend, Vitor Matos has revealed that the injury is not as bad as first thought and he could be in contention to face Chris Davies’ side, as reported by Wales Online.

Matos said: “We will still assess if he will be available for Saturday but it’s not a bad injury. It’s just a knock that is really really painful.”

Welsh has made a great impression since making the switch from Celtic in the summer. Eom Jisung and Moussa Yeo are back available for the Swans this weekend as well, while Ben Cabango is also fit despite a recent knock.

Welsh has quickly established himself as one of Swansea City’s most important players since arriving on a free transfer from Celtic in summer 2026. The 26-year-old signed a two-year contract, with the option of a further year, becoming Vitor Matos’ first summer recruit.

Cameron Burgess’ long-term Achilles injury opened the door for Welsh, who has since made the centre-back position his own. He has started 13 of Swansea’s 14 Championship matches, primarily alongside Ben Cabango, and has impressed with his composure, defensive work and ability to play out from the back.

With Swansea challenging near the top of the table, Welsh has become a key figure. Securing an experienced defender of his quality on a free transfer is proving to be excellent summer business.

Swansea are short of centre-back options without Welsh, although their defensive depth remains manageable in the short term.

Cabango is the obvious leader, while Jenson Seelt offers the most natural replacement after already featuring under Matos. The Sunderland loanee would be the likely partner for Cabango if Welsh is unavailable.

Filip Lissah provides further cover, having demonstrated his versatility by operating centrally, but regularly relying on him could weaken the right-back position. Cameron Burgess remains sidelined with a long-term Achilles injury, leaving Swansea more exposed if Welsh faces an extended absence.

Trump's energy deals are about to face a wave of investigations by Democrats

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump is expected to face a wave of investigations when Democrats take control of Congress in January. His sweeping deal-making in the energy sector is likely to become a central focus.

Democrats have pressed the White House for months on the legality of its intervention in Venezuela's oil sector and its cancellation of renewable energy projects funded by the Biden administration. They have raised conflict of interest concerns over numerous deals in the minerals sector.

But, until now, the opposition party could do little more than send strongly worded letters to administration officials. When the next Congress convenes in January, the Democrats will wield the subpoena power that comes with committee control, giving them the authority to compel testimony and the disclosure of documents.

The House Energy and Commerce Committee in the next Congress intends to pursue an aggressive oversight agenda to root out corruption, Rep. Frank Pallone, the New Jersey Democrat who will likely chair the panel, said in a statement.

With comfortable majorities in the House and Senate, Democrats will likely pass a war powers resolution that demands an end to the conflict, which has hiked fuel prices in the U.S. and around the world.

Crude oil prices sold off sharply Wednesday as the market expected the election defeat to push Trump to cut a deal with Iran. Vice President JD Vance blamed the Republican loss directly on the war, raising questions about whether there is support inside the administration to continue the conflict.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, who will likely take over the House speaker's gavel in January, said Wednesday that Democrats will "end this reckless and costly war of choice that has skyrocketed gas prices all across the country."

The new Congress will try to rein in the administration by passing legislation to stop the conflict, said Rep. Ted Lieu, vice chairman of the Democratic caucus. Jeffries has said Democrats will pass a war powers resolution, though Trump will almost certainly wield his veto. It is unclear whether Democrats will try to restrict funding for the war as well.

Nevertheless, tumbling crude prices could push gasoline below $4 per gallon in the next week and diesel under $6 by Thanksgiving, said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at GasBuddy, in a social media post Wednesday.

But the odds still favor military escalation in the Middle East, warned Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush.

"Iran is hardened in its uncompromising position," McNally said. "Trump, having now paid a huge price for his determination to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon, is unlikely to let go of this legacy goal and walk away."

Democratic lawmakers have pressed the Trump administration for months to provide Congress with an accounting of how Venezuela's oil revenues have been spent — to no avail.

Washington seized control of Venezuela's oil exports in January after ousting former President Nicolás Maduro in a military raid. The Pentagon took a stake in an obscure oil company, North American Blue Energy Partners, or NABEP, that is supposed to develop Venezuelan oil reserves. The deal supposedly gives the U.S. control over 65 billion barrels of oil.

Democratic House members warned the oil industry in January that entering into an agreement with the U.S. on Venezuelan oil could expose the industry to "legal risk, including civil liability from creditors who hold nearly $200 billion worth of debt to Venezuela and their various state-owned entities."

Democratic senators subsequently sent a letter in September to Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Energy Secretary Chris Wright questioning the legal basis for the U.S. government's deal with NABEP.

Energy analysts warned when the NABEP deal was announced in August that it might not survive if Democrats take power in Washington.

Democrats have also raised conflict of interest concerns over the Trump administration's series of deals in the critical minerals sector.

The White House has justified the deals, often involving government equity stakes in private companies, as necessary to support a segment of the mining industry that's critical to U.S. national security and that has been undercut by competitors in China.

But Democratic senators have pointed to the involvement of Cantor Fitzgerald in a number of those deals as cause for concern. Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick ran Cantor before joining the Trump administration. He personally divested from Cantor after joining the Cabinet and turned over control to his sons.

The Democratic senators demanded in a July letter that the federal government preserve any records related to deals involving 14 companies.

For his part, the Commerce secretary dismissed the allegations. "If the Democrats send me letters, I will respond to the letters but they don't matter, since when you do nothing wrong, you don't worry about it and you don't care," Lutnick told CNBC in a September interview."You just answer their silly questions and you move on," Lutnick said. "This is a waste of time trying to create something that doesn't exist."

Others are less cavalier. USA Rare Earth, one of the companies named in the senators' letter, has warned investors that its deal making the U.S. government one of its stockholders has few precedents.

As a consequence, the company could be subject to litigation, political scrutiny and a change in federal priorities, it said in a March filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

The Trump administration has favored fossil fuels and waged a relentless campaign against renewable energy, particularly wind power, since taking office.

Democrats are unlikely to restore tax credits phased out by Trump's big tax bill due to the president's veto power. But they are certain to press the administration on the nearly $8 billion of energy projects it has canceled in states that supported Vice President Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election.

Energy Secretary Chris Wright has told Congress that the projects were not canceled based on political considerations. Several House Democrats have accused Wright of giving false testimony, an allegation the Energy Department has denied.

Democrats are also scrutinizing the administration's efforts to kill offshore wind power in the U.S. The Interior Department has tried to buy out leases for several such projects, for example, paying TotalEnergies nearly $1 billion to cancel an offshore wind project and stop investing in any similar projects in the U.S. in the future. Total agreed to invest in natural gas instead.

Reps. Jared Huffman and Jamie Raskin, the ranking Democrats on the natural resources and judiciary committees, respectively, called the TotalEnergies deal unlawful and launched an investigation in April.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/ardent-health-boosts-2026-guidance-volume-gains-expense-cuts" hreflang="en">Ardent Health boosts 2026 guidance on volume gains, expense cuts</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Ardent Health boosts 2026 guidance on volume gains, expense cuts" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Ardent Health bumped up its 2026 earnings guidance as expense and efficiency improvements met solid “top of the funnel” demand for care during the third quarter, the for-profit shared this week.

The performance comes after a soft second quarter, with executives on Thursday’s earnings call noting that pain points like surgical volumes improved sequentially while broader challenges like Affordable Care Act marketplace dropoffs remain within projections.

Ardent’s shares were trading 20% above open as of Thursday afternoon in spite of Wall Street’s economy-wide selloff.

Leaders said that the company’s Project IMPACT (Improve Margins, Performance, Agility and Care Transformation) initiative is on track to hit “at least” its expected $70 million benefit for 2026, and highlighted a 4% year-over-year reduction in the quarter’s salaries, wages and benefits (SWB) expenses during the quarter driven in part by contract labor expense reduction.

Meanwhile, Ardent is reviewing its portfolio to determine which assets are a long-term strategic fit—leading to decisions like September’s divestiture from a joint venture with the University of Kansas Health System—and plans to deploy its capital toward organic growth in its mid-sized urban markets while maintaining a “disciplined approach” to acquisition or joint venture opportunities that prioritizes above-average potential market growth, CEO Dave Caspar told analysts Thursday.

“When we spoke with you last quarter, I outlined a clear set of priorities: deliver more consistent financial results, grow EBITDA, deploy capital effectively and execute against our commitments in a way that creates long-term shareholder value,” Caspar said during the earnings call. “… Taken together, our third-quarter performance and recent strategic portfolio optimization demonstrate meaningful progress against the priorities I outlined.”

For the third quarter, Ardent reported $1.62 billion in total revenue (3.1% year-over-year growth), $45 million of net income and $130 million in adjusted EBITDA.

The health system's admissions grew by 0.1% and its adjusted admissions by 2.9%, year-over-year improvements over the second quarter, with net patient service revenue per adjusted admission increasing 0.7% since Q3 2025. Inpatient surgeries dropped by 5% and outpatient surgeries by 0.6% compared to the prior-year period, with Chief Financial Officer Alfred Lumsdaine noting that about half of those drops were tied to the health system's "intentional rationalization of certain lower-margin service lines in targeted locations."

Executives said they were happy with the volumes, noting that a 7% drop in exchange admissions and corresponding self-pay increase, similar to Q2’s 8%, was “manageable and largely contemplated in our guidance.” They particularly pointed to strong showings at the top of their “demand funnel,” or areas like urgent care or physician visits that can lead to downstream utilization. Part of the company’s ongoing improvement efforts are focused on boosting access to these offerings while reducing “friction as we move patients through the journey” to capture more care.

“For us, there's a lot of we'll call it embedded growth or backed-up growth that we just need to unlock by reducing friction and ensuring that we're making the process of converting the top of the funnel to the bottom of the funnel easier,” Lumsdaine said.

On the expense side, executives said Project IMPACT helped pull SWB spending down to 39.9% of revenue during the quarter, a 300-basis-point improvement from last year, and slash contract labor from 3.5% of total SWB to 2%. Professional fee expense growth also declined from Q1 2026’s 12.9% to Q3’s 7.4%, with that slowdown expected to slow further in Q4. Supply growth was “a modest 2.6%,” per Lumsdaine.

The performance and expected efficiency gains led Ardent to adjust its full-year 2026 guidance. While revenues are still anticipated to land on “the lower end of our $6.4 billion to $6.7 billion range,” the target for adjusted EBIDTA rose 1.5% at the midpoint.

On expectations for 2027, they declined to outline any specific number targets but noted well-recognized industry headwinds like Medicaid work requirements, marketplace plan attrition, as well as tailwinds like the Project IMPACT performance improvements and the recently finalized outpatient payment rule.

“I'm extremely proud of the progress our team has made this year, despite very fluid industry and macroeconomic dynamics,” Lumsdaine said. “We continue to strengthen our operating model, and we remain focused on building on our performance, generating strong cash flow and creating long-term shareholder value by aligning our people, capital and assets with the highest value opportunities.”

Ardent Health’s operations include 30 acute care hospitals and over 280 care sites, several of which are run as part of a joint venture structure with other health system partners. In 2025, the company increased its revenues by 6% to $6.3 billion and grew its adjusted EBITDA to $545 million.

Greece Approves 10% Crypto Capital Gains Tax as First Framework Lands Below Draft Rate

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Greece's parliament approved a 10% capital gains tax on cryptocurrencies, the country's first dedicated crypto tax framework, with annual gains up to €500 exempt.

Greece’s parliament approved on Thursday a bill that imposes a 10% capital gains tax on cryptocurrencies, the first comprehensive legal framework for taxing crypto assets in the country.

Cryptocurrency capital gains of up to €500 ($563) a year will be exempt from the new tax, the bill says. For bitcoin holders in Greece, that threshold is a small buffer, not a carve-out for larger positions.

“Further implementation directives will be published in the coming weeks, which will also define transparency and audit rules,” a senior finance ministry official told Reuters.

An earlier draft circulated in October had floated a 15% rate. The version lawmakers passed lands five points lower, while keeping the €500 annual exemption that was already in that proposal.

The tax is set to apply from Jan. 1, 2027, Greek coverage of the finance ministry bill says. It is levied on the net gain — the difference between the acquisition price and the transfer price — not on the full sale proceeds. Trading fees and other costs tied to the deal can factor into that calculation.

A taxable transfer is not limited to selling for euros. Using cryptocurrency to buy goods or services, or contributing it to cover or increase company capital, can also crystallize a gain under the new rules. That matters for anyone in Greece who pays a merchant in bitcoin rather than converting first.

Simply holding bitcoin while its euro value rises does not, by itself, create a tax bill. Crypto-to-crypto swaps, such as bitcoin for another token, are treated as non-taxable events in the explanatory material that accompanied the bill, matching the approach France has used to avoid taxing every hop between coins.

Net annual gains at or below €500 are exempt. Greek analyses of the text say that once net gains clear that ceiling, the 10% rate applies to the full net gain for the year, not only the slice above €500. Losses above €500 in a tax year may be carried forward for up to five years and offset only against future crypto capital gains.

Purchases of crypto assets are also being pulled into Greece’s existing “source of funds” checks from 2027, so buyers may need to show that the euros used to acquire bitcoin or other tokens came from declared income or other lawful capital. The rule does not add a separate purchase tax; it widens the audit trail around how coins were funded.

European Union countries do not share a unified tax system for the sector. Rates on cryptocurrency gains typically run from about 8% to 30% across member states and are usually levied on capital gains, the finance ministry official said. Greece’s 10% sits toward the softer end of that band, below France’s often-cited 30% treatment and above Cyprus’s 8%.

The same patchwork is shifting elsewhere in the bloc. The Netherlands has moved toward a realized capital gains tax from 2028, while Germany and Portugal still exempt crypto held longer than a year. Since January, the EU’s DAC8 reporting rules have required crypto platforms to send user and transaction data to national tax authorities — a tool Greece already wrote into domestic law.

State revenue from the new levy could not be projected, the official said, because the vast majority of investors use platforms abroad, leaving the size of Greece’s cryptocurrency market hard to measure.

For bitcoin holders who trade on foreign exchanges, that gap is the practical test. The rate is now set in law; whether Athens can match 10% on paper with enforceable cost-basis data from offshore platforms is the question the coming directives will have to answer.

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Gds - Milan, i 50 mln di Adidas non sono di base. Inter batte cassa con Nike: cifre e scenari

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

L'Inter è pronta a battere cassa con Nike, fa sapere La Gazzetta dello Sport. È uno degli effetti del nuovo contratto tra Milan e Adidas da 50 milioni, che partirà dal 2028 e durerà 10 stagioni. Un accordo importantissimo, visto che l'accordo che i rossoneri avevano con Puma era da 24,5 milioni (da bilancio). La rosea sottolinea che "è logico immaginare che quei 50 milioni incorporino bonus sportivi (per esempio la partecipazione alla Champions), il controvalore della fornitura di materiale (value in kind) e una quota da investire sull’attività promozionale (marketing fund). I contratti con gli sponsor tecnici sono molto complessi perché non si limitano, ovviamente, a riconoscere un valore per la presenza del logo sulle divise da gioco: se l’intesa, come nel caso del Milan, si estende alla vendita dei relativi prodotti, allora entrano in gioco i minimi garantiti e le royalties che scattano oltre una certa soglia di fatturato.

[...] Alla luce di ciò, è molto complicato fare un confronto con le altre squadre. In ogni caso, ancor prima di questa notizia-bomba sul Milan, l’Inter risultava essere sottovalutata nella sua partnership tecnica, considerata la crescita compiuta sul piano sportivo e commerciale. Negli ultimi quattro anni sono arrivati due scudetti e due finali di Champions e il club ha aumentato di 86 milioni i ricavi annui da stadio, sponsorizzazioni e merchandising: nel 2025-26 la prima voce è stata pari a 92 milioni (79 nel 2022-23), le altre due, ricadenti nell’area commerciale, a 151 milioni (78 nel 2022-23). Sono stati sottoscritti accordi lucrativi e strategici con Betsson, Bbva, Byd, Coca-Cola, Red Bull e Budweiser.

[...] Il rinnovo con Nike, in vigore dal 2023, ha sì portato a un incremento del 70% rispetto al contratto precedente, ma i 28 milioni annui non sono in linea con l’attuale valore commerciale del club e con il benchmark di riferimento: in attesa che si concretizzi il matrimonio Milan-Adidas, al momento la Juventus percepisce dalla stessa casa tedesca 46 milioni annui (41 dal 2027-28). È vero che l’Inter ha mantenuto al proprio interno i ricavi del merchandising, che nel 2025-26 hanno superato i 35 milioni grazie al nuovo modello di business introdotto con Fanatics.

Ma è indubitabile che i 28 milioni di Nike siano troppo pochi, a giudizio del management nerazzurro: l’intesa scade nel 2031 e l’Inter cercherà di rinegoziare il contratto per evitare di rimanere indietro per altri cinque anni. Volendo guardare il bicchiere mezzo pieno, con le altre big già vincolate per il prossimo decennio (Adidas è legata alla Juve fino al 2037 e sarà legata al Milan fino al 2038), l’Inter resterà l’unica grande property italiana libera per chiunque vorrà entrare", si legge su Gazzetta.it.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/cigna-beats-street-14b-profit-737b-revenue-q3" hreflang="en">Cigna offers investors a look at its new Lead to One strategy</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Cigna offers investors a look at its new Lead to One strategy" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Cigna offered investors additional color on its new healthcare transformation strategy on Thursday morning.

First unveiled at the company's investor day on Sept. 30, the Lead to One initiative aims to bring together the array of assets under the Cigna Group's umbrella—from medical and pharmacy benefits to behavioral health to specialty care—to address the individual needs of members and patients.

CEO Brian Evanko said during Cigna's earnings call that the program aims to lean on data analytics and technology to identify risks more quickly and deliver personalized supports. The goal, he said, is to drive better outcomes while lowering healthcare costs in tandem.

"Our Lead to One vision will benefit all customers, and we see a particular opportunity with the most clinically complex patient needs, where healthcare costs are increasingly concentrated and represent over 50% of total healthcare spending," he said.

Evanko said that the approach helps to establish "clear strategic growth framework for creating long-term value."

"It is an exciting time here at The Cigna Group as our company unifies around a single vision, Lead to One, and begins to compound the distinctive capabilities we have developed to capture the opportunity set in front of us," he said.

In addition, the company is undertaking an initiative called Accelerate to One, which aims to support operational efficiency for its workforce while supporting greater accountability and better decision-making. Evanko said that program is set to drive $3 billion in run rate savings for the company by 2030.

Evanko said the company expects to see those savings opportunities materialize across the company.

Cigna is the latest health insurance company to boost its guidance for the year on the back of its Q3 results.

The company released its earnings report Thursday morning, posting $1.4 billion in profit. That's down year over year from the $1.9 billion haul the company reported in the third quarter of 2025.

In addition, revenues for the quarter were $73.7 billion, up from $69.7 billion in the prior-year quarter. Both figures surpassed Wall Street analysts' predictions, per Zacks Investment Research.

Through the first three quarters of the year, the company has earned $4.8 billion in profit, on par with the $4.7 billion posted for the first nine months of 2025. Total revenue at the end of Q3 was $213.8 billion, growing from $202.4 billion through three quarters in 2025.

CEO Brian Evanko said in the release that the company's current financial strategy is rooted in three key principles, including "driving core growth, leveraging our distinct capabilities and executing with discipline."

"As we put our strategy in action, our strong third quarter results demonstrate how we are building on this momentum by making health care more affordable and personalized, while advancing Accelerate to One to drive productivity, reduce costs, and support sustained long-term earnings growth," he said.

The company reported a medical loss ratio in the quarter of 85%, up slightly from 84.8% in Q3 2025. Cigna attributed the uptick to higher costs in its employer plans, while lower costs in the individual market segment did partially offset this trend.

Cigna Healthcare, the company's core insurance business, reported 18.45 million members as of Q3. By comparison, the insurer had 18.1 million members at the end of 2025.

Pharmacy benefit membership, meanwhile, was 117.7 million, down 123.6 million as of Dec. 31, 2025. Cigna said the decrease reflects "expected client transitions" as well as lower membership at its health plan clients.

Its Evernorth unit reported $63.8 billion in adjusted revenue for the quarter, up from $61.5 billion a year ago. Highlights include ongoing organic growth in its specialty pharmacy business, the company said.

Cigna Healthcare reported $11.7 billion in adjusted revenue for Q3, up from $10.7 billion in the prior-year quarter. The company said this largely reflects higher premium revenue as it adapts to higher medical costs.

On the back of the results, Cigna elevated its outlook and now expects at least $30.60 in earnings per share for 2026. It also projects a full-year MLR of 84.2%.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Telling our children about your net worth - pros and cons?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our kids are grown, responsible and doing well. We shared the information with them a few years ago when we started making gifts up to the gift tax exemption limit. In the years since, the kids have not gone crazy with buying junk or become demotivated or whatever other fears folks seem to have. We helped one set up an account at Vanguard so the gifts could be investments instead of cash. They now make regular contributions to the account on their own as well. They've taken our coaching to just use low cost index funds and avoid all our investing mistakes.

So it's all about the kids being self-motivated enough that it doesn't derail them.

Illegal gambling ring busted after using Pokemon cards as payouts

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Police in Texas have busted an alleged illegal gambling operation that was using Pokemon and sports trading cards to pay out winnings instead of cash.

Pokemon cards have become increasingly valuable in recent years, with rare collectibles selling for thousands or even millions of dollars. However, authorities in San Antonio believe one group of businesses was taking advantage of their value for an entirely different reason.

According to police, customers who won money on gambling machines were instead handed valuable trading cards, which they could then exchange for cash at a nearby business.

The unusual arrangement came to light after officers raided seven locations on November 4, detaining 23 people and seizing hundreds of gambling machines.

Bexar County Sheriff Javier Salazar explained that the businesses, which operated under the name “777,” were allegedly attempting to get around Texas gambling laws.

Under state rules, certain gaming establishments are restricted in the value of prizes they can award. Rather than paying customers large sums directly, the businesses allegedly used Pokemon and sports cards as an alternative.

Winners could then take their cards to a neighboring store, which would purchase them for cash.

Illegal Pokemon Gambling Ring BUSTEDCredit: Jbfromsaen IG pic.twitter.com/gAVQNRsSnw

Speaking to the San Antonio Express-News, Salazar explained how customers could receive a card and sell it to collect their winnings.

“So here’s your sports cards that you won for gambling with us, you can go next door and sell this to them, and they’ll give you a couple of hundred dollars for it,” Salazar said. “We know what they’re doing, they’re trying to skirt the law, it doesn’t make it any less illegal.”

Pokemon cards seized as part of massive drug bust in Australia

Police uncover $100K in rare Pokemon & MTG cards after thieves steal 18 golf carts

Pokemon scammers arrested for using bags of paper to buy $320K worth of cards

The raids took place across northern and western San Antonio, with authorities confiscating between 500 and 600 gambling machines.

Of the 23 people detained, 13 were expected to face charges or be jailed over alleged offenses including drug possession, operating illegal gambling establishments, and organized criminal activity.

American Airlines Quietly Removes Trump From West Palm Beach Gate Monitors

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

American Airlines has updated the gate monitors for flights to West Palm Beach, and they no longer mention President Trump. This seems like a sensible change (unrelated to politics), and the timing sure is… interesting.

Over the summer, we saw Palm Beach International Airport (PBI) renamed President Donald J. Trump International Airport. We knew that Trump wanted an airport named after him, and some conservative politicians in Florida came together to make it happen.

It’s interesting to see how airlines handled this. At the time, American updated its gate monitors for flights to West Palm Beach. The monitors initially read “West Palm Beach, FL.” American then updated the monitors to say “Pres DJT, W Palm Beach.” Now JonNYC reports that as of this week, the airport name listed on the monitors has been changed again, and is back to the original name, with no mention of Trump.

so… has anyone noticed that AA has changed the gate monitors from “Pres DJT, W Palm Beach” back to “West Palm Beach, FL” ? https://t.co/IG2mPA3zgG

JonNYC specifically emphasizes that he’s not claiming there’s any connection between this and the election results, though the timing of it happening this week sure is interesting.

Personally I’m not a fan of airports being named after even semi-controversial figures, and particularly politicians. It’s something I’ve said over and over, regardless of the party affiliation of the person. Unless a city has multiple airports, I think it’s also stupid to identify the airport based on the name rather than the place.

Like, it makes sense to clarify London Heathrow (LHR) vs. London Gatwick (LGW), but it’s not like there are multiple airports in West Palm Beach. For that matter, saying you’re “flying to President Trump” just sounds a little weird, in my opinion.

JonNYC notes that he’s not suggesting that this had anything to do with the recent election, though everything about the timing here is interesting:

American Airlines CEO Robert Isom: "From a commercial aviation perspective, the eyes of the world have been on us, and we've certainly delivered… No doubt, it's a result of the economy and the job market." https://t.co/28JzoEf4Z3 pic.twitter.com/BcgYJbChNz

American Airlines seems to have quietly reversed course on how it refers to West Palm Beach’s airport on its gate monitors. In recent months, the airline has referred to the airport as “Pres DJT, W Palm Beach,” while now it seems to be back to “West Palm Beach, FL.”

This change comes the same week as the midterm elections, though everyone can decide for themselves to what extent these points are related…

What do you make of this American gate monitor change?

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: When the market tanks, what do you plan to do in retirement?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I won’t (can’t) do much in a crash.

I’m only drawing ~1% per year and can’t go too much lower, of course if markets skyrocket that draw % will drop but I’ll still be taking the same number of dollars.

I do have an emergency fund in CDs so I could buy some more stock but I’m still of the school that an EF is a good idea.

I can’t shift bond $$$ into stocks because my yield will drop, yield is kinda what keeps me out of trouble in a crash.

So I guess I’m somewhat locked in.

Lamar Jackson's latest injury raises obvious questions about his future in Baltimore

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With quarterback Lamar Jackson’s ninth NFL season possibly over after three full games and two first-half exits, obvious questions have emerged regarding his future in Baltimore.

The facts fuel the speculation. He’s under contract for one more season, and the Ravens can’t use the franchise tag or the transition tag to limit his ability to become an unrestricted free agent in March 2028, which is only 16 months away.

If Jackson wants a fresh start at age 31, he’ll be able to have one.

The Ravens made it clear in January that they wanted to extend his contract by the start of the new league year in March 2026. It didn’t happen, for reasons that never became clear because neither Jackson nor the Ravens (at Jackson’s behest) share information about the negotiations.

Before the season began, it seemed that a new contract wa just a matter of time. The Ravens surely would like to do it before the start of the new league year in March, when Jackson’s cap number spikes to $84.34 million. Although the team has the right to do another restructuring that would reduce the 2027 cap number, that would make the dead-cap reckoning even larger as of 2028.

A trade would avoid the final $52 million in cash and cap charges, but the cap realities would make a post-June 1 move the far better option. And there have been whispers of at least one team doing advance homework on a potential pursuit of Jackson in 2027.

The overriding question is whether Jackson’s lingering availability issue changes things for the Ravens. For now, there’s no reason to think it has. When the dust settles on the season, the Ravens will have to revisit that question.

But that will lead to another question. What would they do at quarterback without Lamar Jackson? That simple reality prompted them to give him a market-level deal in 2023, and to want to do it again.

As long as that desire to avoid a future without Lamar Jackson remains, there’s no reason to think he won’t be back in Baltimore in 2027.

Why isn't Lamar Jackson playing today? Injury update, return timeline for Ravens QB

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Baltimore Ravens are taking on the Jacksonville Jaguars in a "Thursday Night Football" game that could prove crucial for their chances of staying in the AFC North race.

The Ravens will have to navigate that game, and the foreseeable future, without the services of their starting quarterback, Lamar Jackson.

Jackson suffered a thumb injury that is expected to sideline him long-term. This marks the second time this season the veteran quarterback is facing a multi-game absence, after missing three games leading up to Baltimore's Week 8 game against the Buffalo Bills with an ankle injury.

Here's everything you need to know about Jackson's thumb injury and when he might return to the field.

Jackson suffered a thumb injury in the Ravens' Week 8 loss to the Buffalo Bills.

The injury occurred on Jackson's first passing attempt of the game, when he hit his throwing hand on the arm of Bills pass rusher Gregory Rousseau. Jackson immediately reached for his hand in pain and was seen clutching it while running toward the sideline.

Jackson was quickly deemed out for the remainder of the game. He was later spotted wearing a cast on his injured hand after he returned to the field clad in street clothes during the second half of the contest.

Before the game against Buffalo, Jackson had missed the previous three games because of an ankle injury. He has appeared in five of the Ravens' eight games this season but has only finished three.

The Ravens have not provided a definitive timeline for Jackson's return, but coach Jesse Minter confirmed the star quarterback will be placed on IR because of his thumb injury.

"That'll be a surgery deal," Minter said when discussing Jackson's injury during a postgame news conference on Sunday, Nov. 1. "He'll go to IR. That's a tough deal."

Players placed on IR must miss a minimum of four games, per NFL rules. That would guarantee Jackson misses the Ravens' Weeks 9-12 games before becoming eligible to return for their Week 14 contest against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, which comes after their Week 13 bye.

But Minter did acknowledge that Jackson's injury could "potentially" be season-ending. He also declined to provide updates about the quarterback's thumb when asked during a Monday, Nov. 2 news conference.

"I'm not going to get into the specifics of the injury," Minter told reporters.

Minter did reveal Jackson had not yet had surgery to repair his thumb malady and that the Ravens would likely have a more concrete update about his status "maybe sometime this week."

The Ravens have not yet clarified whether Jackson will be able to return during the 2026 NFL season. Minter simply said the injury was "potentially" season-ending but did not offer a concrete timeline for the veteran quarterback's return.

Normally, veteran Tyler Huntley would take on that mantle in Jackson's absence. However, he is dealing with a turf toe injury and is expected to miss the rest of the season because of it.

That has left third-string quarterback Joe Fagnano to serve as the starter for "Thursday Night Football" in Week 9. The undrafted rookie spent the final three seasons of his college career at UConn and completed 68% of his passes for 3,448 yards, 28 touchdowns and one interception during his sixth and final college season.

Fagnano (6-3, 230 pounds) completed 10 of 21 passes for 91 yards and an interception in relief of Jackson and Huntley against the Bills.

That said, Fagnano isn't expected to be the Ravens' long-term starter. The team has agreed to acquire veteran quarterback Jacoby Brissett in a trade with the Arizona Cardinals, and Brissett will assume the starting mantle beginning in Week 10, if all goes well.

Brissett has previous experience working with Ravens quarterback coach Israel Woolfork during their time together with the Arizona Cardinals.

Baltimore's quarterback depth chart remains in flux with Jackson and Huntley out and Brissett not yet officially acquired.

Brissett is expected to top the Ravens' quarterback depth chart once he officially joins the team, but below is a look at the pecking order within the group for Thursday's game.

Reed signed to the Ravens' practice squad on Monday, Nov. 2, just after Jackson and Huntley's injury. The 2024 undrafted free agent previously played for the Chicago Bears but has never appeared in NFL regular-season game. The Western Kentucky product thrived for the Dallas Renegades during the 2026 UFL season, completing 56.3% of his passes for 1,923 yards, a league-high 21 touchdowns and nine interceptions.

Slovis is another player who went undrafted in 2024. The USC, Pitt and BYU product spent time with four NFL teams before joining the Ravens from the Packers' practice squad. He dressed for two games with the Arizona Cardinals last season and completed 1 of 2 passes for negative-2 yards.

Because Slovis was on the Packers' practice squad, the Ravens needed to sign him to their 53-man roster to obtain him ahead of Thursday's game. That said, the 25-year-old will likely remain behind Reed given that the latter spent time with Baltimore during the preseason – though Slovis, like Brissett, previously played for Woolfork in Arizona.

Angie Nixon could have won Florida. National Democrats failed her

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Election Day brought a big blue earthquake of anti-Trump sentiment that rumbled as far inland as Kansas and shook GOP ground in the Carolinas and Texas. By the time late victories rolled in on Tuesday night, there had been so many wins that subsequent Democratic pickups had begun to feel more like aftershocks, even in states that havenât voted blue since Bill Clinton was in the White House.

Florida narrowly missed the quake, with Republicans winning all the major statewide races by the thinnest of margins. The one-time quintessential purple state, a so-goes-the-nation bellwether with a relatively even amount of registered Republicans and Democrats, turned remarkably red in the Trump era following Barack Obamaâs back-to-back wins in 2008 and 2012. The state legislature redrew its maps to enshrine a supermajority, and a post-Covid influx of GOP transplants seeking lower taxes and lax regulations shifted the balance significantly. A Democrat hasnât made a fight of the governorâs race this decade, and no Democratic Senate candidate has come close to winning since Bill Nelson lost his reelection bid in 2018.

Angie Nixon felt different. The Jacksonville organizer and bookstore owner ran a proudly democratic socialist campaign, advocating for universal healthcare in a state beset by multiple insurance crises and affordability issues. Her polls looked good up to Election Day, and on Tuesday night, victories across the country made her race in the Sunshine State look winnable. Ultimately though, Nixon could not replicate the victories of other rabble-rousers like Dr. Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan and James Talarico in Texas. She lost to incumbent Ashley Moody, who was appointed by Gov. Ron DeSantis to fill the seat left vacant by Marco Rubioâs confirmation as secretary of state, by less than two percentage points. Moody outspent Nixon by more than double to barely eke out a majority.Â

The Florida Democratic Party deserves some blame for being woefully behind the times. After a 2019 squeaker between DeSantis and the progressive Andrew Gillum, the party moved as fast as it could from any candidate that might excite its base. They ran a former Republican for governor in each of the last two gubernatorial races, no doubt depressing turnout and enthusiasm among the Democratic base with their moderate picks.Â

But a close look at the rest of the Florida races reveals the culprit in Nixonâs narrow loss. Despite a gerrymander meant to calcify Republican supermajorities in the state and a political stiff in the form of David Jolly at the top of the ballot for governor, the GOP lost seats in the state legislature. The energy in Florida and, remarkably, throughout the South, was with anyone running against Republicans. The blame for Nixonâs defeat lies almost solely with national Democrats and their stubborn refusal to back Nixon with either messaging or money.Â

Since her shock primary win in August, Nixon has complained about Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee recalcitrance to anyone who will listen. In September, she told MS NOW hosts Symone Sanders Townsend and Eugene Daniels that Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer was less than thrilled about her race.

âWhen I did speak with Sen. Schumer on the phone, it was basically a âwe will see,ââ she said. âHeâs going to look into it. Thatâs what was said, that heâs going to look into it.â

Nixon went on to say that Democrats were wary of throwing in behind a Black woman in the South following Kamala Harrisâ 2024 loss: âFolks are a little gun-shy because, unfortunately, Vice President Harris lost as a Black woman, and so people are being very hesitant in terms of funding Black women, especially in the South.â

In later interviews, she claimed that national Democrats wrote off Florida in advance, declining to put money behind her candidacy because they believe the state is âracist.âÂ

The pale blue dots on Floridaâs election map tell the story. The democratic socialist was able to win in Miami-Dade County, despite a Cuban-American population that shudders at anything that sounds like revolution. On the other end of the state, typically Republican Jacksonville swung to the left. An enthusiastic push in the stateâs timidly liberal cities could have narrowed the gap in favor of Nixon and added tens of thousands of votes to her final total. According to analysis by Wesleyan Media Project, the national party and affiliated PACs spent 10 advertising dollars in Iowa for every one they spent in Florida in the raceâs final weeks, and it showed. The Hawkeye State now has a new Democratic governor-elect and senator-elect.

It was the same story throughout the South. Democrats overperformed in states where the national party bothered to fight.

Blue Texas became a reality because of seemingly unlimited spending â it was the most expensive Senate race in history âÂ and national messaging hits on behalf of Talarico. Louisiana and Mississippi offered two incredibly close Senate races in states long assumed to be GOP strongholds. In Louisiana, Democrat Jamie Davis lost to Republican Julia Letlow by only two points. As New York Times contributing opinion writer Steven Rattner pointed out, Davis kept the race close despite receiving zero advertising dollars from the national party in October, per the WMPâs analysis.

Mississippi, though, could turn out to be a miracle â in spite of the DSCC. With 96% of precincts reporting, Democrat Scott Colom, a district attorney, leads the incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith by only 138 votes, with around 15,000 provisional ballots outstanding.

Speaking to Anderson Cooper on Wednesday, Mississippi state Rep. Cheikh Taylor, a Democrat, blamed the nail-biter on a lack of investment from the national party.

âEarly money always helps. Monies have come to the state of Mississippi, just not through the traditional means, though the DNC. Donât get me wrong. Colom, Davis and Nixon all deserve praise for their stunning performances, even if at least two of them came up short. Tuesday nightâs news offered a rare glimmer of hope in a bleak few years, but Democrats had a chance to permanently rebuke MAGA âÂ and instead they squandered it based on their own misconceptions about the South.

If the Senate races in Mississippi and Alaska both go to the Democratic candidates, the party will be only four votes shy of the supermajority they need to overcome Donald Trumpâs veto power. No amount of spending would have pushed Democrats over that 60 vote threshold. But grabbing two more close races via some well-placed funding for the partyâs nominees would have left them in a much better position to harry and harass the president and block his MAGA agenda over the next two years. All it would have taken was not capitulating in advance.

The Fate of Stephen Graham's Perfect Crime Series Has Officially Been Decided

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lade Omotade is a News and Features Author at Collider, covering everything from casting announcements and streaming trends to behind-the-scenes stories and the cultural impact of today's biggest film and TV franchises. Since joining the team in 2024, she has written more than 1,000 news stories, with her work also cited by outlets like AOL and IMDb.

Only a week ago, Stephen Graham's Apple TV crime drama premiered with two gripping episodes, both of which have been well received by fans and critics alike. Created by Rowan Joffé as an adaptation of Lars Kepler's bestselling crime novels Lazarus and The Sandman, Nocturne is the 10/10 series currently taking over Apple TV globally. The psychological thriller is expected to conclude its first season after ten episodes on December 25, 2026, and has not yet been officially renewed for a second season.

In Nocturne, Graham delivers a compelling performance as Jurek Walter, a serial killer incarcerated in the basement of Riverside Psychiatric Hospital. He is joined by Liev Schreiber and Zazie Beetz, who portray Jonah Lynn, a former homicide detective, and Saga Bauer, an undercover FBI agent and Jonah's surrogate daughter, respectively. Graham's portrayal recalls his performance in a similarly unsettling crime series where he once again demonstrates his remarkable ability to bring depth and vulnerability to morally complex characters.

The BBC anthology prison drama Time, created and written by Jimmy McGovern, sees Graham portray Eric McNally, a highly respected veteran prison officer whose moral compass is shattered by tragic circumstances. The series premiered on BBC One on June 6, 2021, and ran for three seasons, concluding on October 18, 2026. Meanwhile, in the U.S., it wraps up Season 3 on November 5 on BritBox, and many have wondered whether it will return for a fourth run. Series creator McGovern has finally provided an answer during a recent chat with TV Insider.

Britain makes more detective television than any country has any business making, and somehow almost all of it is good. Here is the one you belong in, what it says about you, and the case for it being the best of the four.

You want the tea, the gossip and the vicar behaving oddly. Midsomer is a county where the scenery is beautiful, the fete is on Saturday and the murder rate is roughly that of a small war, and absolutely nobody involved thinks this is strange.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

According to McGovern, Time won't return for Season 4 because he believes there's no story left to tell. "I don't think there will be one," the showrunner said. "I think we've done men's, we've done women's, and we've done the young, so there's nothing else really." He also revealed that the original plan was to do only one season in a men's prison from the start. Apparently, the BBC didn't expect the show to become very popular; hence, the success led to Seasons 2 and 3.

“When I first set off, it was only going to be one series of three episodes in a men’s prison. I know what the BBC were expecting. They were expecting it to be fairly well received, but not popular, because that’s my stuff. It was well received, and it was massively popular. So only after it was shown did they say to me, ‘Can we do another one, please?’”

As an anthology, Time follows the prisoners, guards, and chaplain at a men's prison in Season 1, a women's prison in Season 2, and a young offenders institution in Season 3. The cast changes each season: Season 1 stars Graham and Sean Bean; Season 2 stars Jodie Whittaker, Tamara Lawrance, and Bella Ramsey; and Season 3 stars David Tennant. Siobhan Finneran has remained the prison chaplain, Mary-Louise, across all three seasons.

Time streams on BritBox, while Nocturne streams on Apple TV.

Could Archer Aviation Stock Make You a Millionaire by 2036?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A multitrillion-dollar opportunity in aviation, an eVTOL that ferries passengers in the sky: Could Archer mint new millionaires?

The 2030s could become one of the most momentous decades of recorded history, for more reasons than one. Those years could be the first full decade in which we have artificial general intelligence (AGI), or something close to it. That would not only accelerate the pace of scientific and technological breakthroughs, but quite possibly shift the economic foundations on which society is based (e.g., robots in the workforce).

Another change in the next decade could come to transportation: Indeed, if Archer Aviation (ACHR -0.24%) has its way, by 2036, a large portion of travelers and commuters will be flying in air taxis instead of driving cars.

But can Archer make and certify eVTOLs at scale by 2036? And better yet, could its stock, currently trading almost 60% lower since last year, rebound and make investors millionaires?

In a nutshell, Archer is in the business of making eVTOL (electric vertical takeoff and landing) aircraft. Today, that business is mostly nonexistent (or rather, it exists in a pre-commercialization form). Indeed, no eVTOL is flying commercially right now in the U.S. But by 2036, this industry could be at a breakout point, one that transforms eVTOLs into a multibillion-dollar commercial market.

Morgan Stanley, for instance, projected in a 2021 report that the global total addressable market (TAM) for urban air mobility, a broader industry that includes eVTOLs, could grow from $55 billion in 2030 to $255 billion in 2035, before reaching a staggering $1 trillion by 2040. Morgan Stanley even went so far as to project a $9 trillion market by 2050.

Of course, this doesn't mean that Archer will grow into a $1 trillion company by 2040, or that it will even capture a large portion of that growing market. Indeed, Morgan Stanley's forecast encompasses several markets -- like cargo and military -- not just passenger transportation.

But since Archer is also developing military aircraft through its partnership with Anduril, and has plans to acquire a defense business (Insitu) from Boeing, its growth opportunity could well exceed ferrying passengers around cities.

Here's a thought: If Archer doesn't have a flying taxi business in 2036, can it survive as a company?

I think so. With its planned acquisitions from Boeing, it could lean more heavily on defense. It could also find opportunities selling autonomous aircraft for other purposes, like transporting cargo or carrying out military missions. But without a successful air taxi business, I doubt Archer will grow large enough to make millionaires out of investors buying shares in 2026.

Still, just because Archer doesn't mint millionaires doesn't mean it's off the table as a growth stock. But just know what you're buying -- a company with a lot to prove -- and understand the risks of investing in a speculative aviation company before you do.

Steven Porrello has positions in Archer Aviation. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Boeing. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Linehouse reimagines industrial aesthetics for Jen Hangzhou hotel in Kohn Pedersen Fox-designed building

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Photography by Jonathan Leijonhufvud and Wen Studio

International studio Linehouse has completed the interiors of the Jen hotel in Hangzhou, China, which features details that nod to local craft and the area’s industrial heritage.

The hotel, which is owned by the Shangri-La Hotel Group, is located along Hangzhou’s Dongxin River in an urban setting and flanked by two former industrial factory compounds.

The 18-floor hotel building was designed by US architecture firm Kohn Pedersen Fox, using steel, grey brick and glass to reference the site’s industrial past.

The same design approach was extended into the interior by Linehouse, which focuses on showcasing local craft and nods to the industrial elements of the former factories.

“Jen Hangzhou is an urban hotel that celebrates its neighbourhood context – one that layers history, craft, culture and rich materiality into a new perspective on Hangzhou,” Linehouse said.

The studio used earth-toned marble, oak panelling and relaxed furniture in the ground-floor lobby to create a calm and warm atmosphere.

At the entrance, guests are greeted by a textured artwork by artist Elsa Jeandedieu that depicts an abstract version of the bridges and canal close to the hotel.

Patchwork patterns on the marble floor and on a bespoke rug in the lobby were informed by the street layout and tile patterns of the surrounding neighbourhood.

All the hospitality spaces, including a restaurant, a bar and function rooms are located on the sixth floor.

The long, linear OpenHouse restaurant is wrapped by full-height glazing that invites natural light inside.

A timber design feature spans the ceiling and was informed by the craft of sunmao, a traditional timber joinery technique. It is highlighted by a thin black metal frame that supports suspended lighting.

At the centre of the restaurant, a coffee bar splits the space into a dining area on one side and an all-day lounge on the other.

In the private dining room, the timber canopy has an angled design and is decorated with burnt red and yellow hues. In the rest of the OpenHouse restaurant, rooms feature teal tiles and handmade ceramic wall lighting.

The adjacent Koan Bar is a triple-height venue characterised by an undulating black metal staircase that takes guests to a terrace on the upper floor, with incorporated platforms where guests can socialise.

The sinuous staircase is carved between walls lined with concrete panels, another nod to the site’s industrial heritage.

Beneath it, curved booths in walnut timber and tan leather provide intimate seating spaces.

Three interconnected spaces on the sixth floor, enveloped by undulating timber walls, will be used for events and intimate social gatherings. Like in the lobby, bespoke carpets feature patterns abstracted from urban details in the neighbourhood.

The seventh floor is dedicated to wellness and comprises a gym, a yoga room, a spa, a pool and changing rooms, made from tactile materials with muted tones to create a calm environment.

At the gym, a lava stone wall has engravings that illustrate movement, while the yoga room features hand-chiseled timber panels.

A sloped skylit annex accommodates the swimming pool.

The 176 guest rooms were conceived as “urban retreats”. Here, Linehouse used a restrained palette of gun metal, oak datum, textured render and tan leather to underline the industrial aesthetic.

“The public areas explore traditional craft techniques such as sunmao timber joinery, clay tiling and bricks, natural patterns, textures and renders, whilst the guestrooms explore a refined industrial aesthetic,” Linehouse said.

“Red brick, concrete, metal and bold forms reference the surrounding industrial environment whilst handcrafted tiles, vintage timber, colours and metals with different patinas introduce warmth, richness and texture.”

The guest rooms have curved ceilings that create indirect lighting and add softness to the space, while the bathrooms are illuminated by glass-block walls.

Founded by Alex Mok and Briar Hickling in 2013, Linehouse is an architecture and interior practice with studios in Shanghai, Hong Kong and Auckland.

It has recently designed Ink Ink Market in Shanghai with a translucent sloping facade, as well as the interiors for Bangkok’s Central Park shopping centre with three dramatic atriums, which was shortlisted of Retail Interior (large) by this year’s Dezeen Awards.

Area: 12,850 sqm Client: Kerry Properties Operator: Shangri-La Design principal: Alex Mok Design team: Wang Jue, Jingru Tong, Yunbin Lou, Cherngyu Chen, Inez Low, Yeling Guo, Aiwen Shao, Mia Zhou, Norman Wang, Yuechen Wu Lighting: LPA Decorative lighting: Kris Lite, Van Collection, Yi Design, Yun Zhao Studio, Fine Lumens, Santa & Cole, Audo, Timothy Oulton, La Nuit Design consultant: EDGE Design Interior contractor: Terart Decoration Design & Engineering Co., Ltd, Shenzhen Lead management consultant: Wong & Ouyang (HK) Ltd. LDI & structural consultant: Zhejiang Province Institute of Architectural Design and Research MEP engineer consultant: Hangzhou MJP Mechanical & Electrical Design Consultant Ltd.

World's largest olive oil company plummets 18% after rivals agree to takeover deal

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Shares of Deoleo, the world's largest olive oil company, plummeted Thursday after two rivals announced plans to acquire the company, a deal that would further consolidate Spain's dominance in the sector.

Spanish olive oil firms Dcoop and Acesur will each hold a 50% stake in the company, resulting in a new olive oil giant in the region.

The deal is intended to boost olive oil consumption worldwide and support farmers in creating value through the supply chain, Dcoop said in an announcement.

Deoleo was last trading down 17.8%. However the stock is up around 55% since the beginning of the year as it's benefited from a moderation in olive oil prices that has helped lift U.S. sales.

CNBC reached out to Deoleo for comment and is awaiting a response.

The news comes after an intensifying takeover battle between Dcoop and multinational firms including those from Italy, France, and Australia. The stock gained over 20% on Aug. 19 when news of the takeover battle emerged. Dcoop reportedly previously offered 470 million euros ($545 million) for Deoleo.

Spain is one of the world's leading olive oil producers and is often used as a global reference for prices.

Prices for the commodity have been volatile over the past few years as supplies swung dramatically one season to the next, with climate change, water scarcity, and pest and disease playing a role.

Deoleo CEO Cristóbal Valdés told CNBC in July that the period between 2022 and 2024 was one of the most challenging periods for the sector, but that prices are now stabilizing due to favourable rain trends.

"The highly complex market cycle experienced between 2022 and 2024, which had a severe yet temporary impact on the industry, is now definitively behind us," Valdés said at the time.

North Korea tells EU to 'mind its own business' on human rights

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

North Korea's UN mission has lashed out at the European Union over a draft UN resolution. Pyongyang has said the West does not have a monopoly on human rights.

North Korea, responded European Union's draft United Nations resolution on human rights in the country by telling the bloc to "mind its own business," state media reported Thursday.

Pyongyang has long been accused by the UN and human rights advocates of committing human rights violations.

North Korea's UN mission said in a statement carried by the official Korean Central News Agency that the move was a "farce" and a "politically motivated provocation."

It said the resolution is "flagrantly distorting it's human rights policy," claiming that human rights are not "a monopoly of the West."

Referring to the protests in Europe, "Streets are filled with demonstrators from all walks of life demanding such elementary rights as the rights to existence," the North Korean mission said.

"The EU had better mind its own business, instead of hyping up the non-existent 'human rights issues,'" it added.

The UN General Assembly has adopted a resolution condemning North Korea's human rights violations every year since 2005.

According to the South Korean Yonhap News Agency, Seoul has decided to co-sponsor the resolution proposed by the EU.

It is the fifth consecutive year that Seoul has done so, following a break from 2019 to 2021 under the liberal Moon Jae-in government.

The draft text is expected to be adopted by the Third Committee of the UN General Assembly later this month, before being submitted to the UN General Assembly for final approval.

Scientists Sound the Alarm As Colorectal Cancer Reverses a 20-Year Decline

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Colorectal cancer rates in the United States are rising across a much wider range of ages than previously recognized, reversing a long period of declining diagnoses. A new study published in NEJM Evidence found that the increase is no longer concentrated among adults younger than 50, with rates now climbing in people approaching their 70s.

Colorectal cancer is the second leading cause of cancer-related deaths overall in the United States and the leading cause among adults younger than 50. As more generations reach ages when the disease becomes increasingly common, researchers warn that growing numbers of diagnoses could place additional demands on cancer treatment and long-term survivorship care.

Researchers led by Caitlin Murphy, PhD, MPH, Professor of Pediatrics and Public Health Sciences at the University of Chicago, analyzed National Cancer Institute data to track changes in colorectal cancer incidence over more than two decades. The age-adjusted rate dropped from 55.2 cases per 100,000 people in 2000 to 36.7 in 2022, before increasing to 38.3 in 2023.

“This is the first time that we have seen an increase in the overall rate since in more than 20 years,” Murphy said. The study also found rising incidence across age groups through 65 to 69 years, indicating that the trend extends well beyond the younger populations that have received much of the attention.

The increase among older adults is particularly concerning because colorectal cancer screening has historically produced substantial benefits in these age groups. Rates are now rising among people ages 65 to 69 and have stopped declining among those ages 70 to 74, despite approximately 80% of people in these groups being up to date with screening. Researchers suspect that unidentified risk factors may be counteracting some of the progress achieved through earlier detection and prevention.

Murphy believes the expanding age range reflects a phenomenon known as the “birth cohort effect,” in which people born during particular periods experience different disease risks as they grow older. In this case, evidence suggests that individuals born in the 1960s and afterward may face an elevated risk of colorectal cancer that persists as they advance into middle and older adulthood.

“I think of a birth cohort effect as the interaction of age and time,” Murphy explained. “As people born around the same time move through time and age together, we’re going to see more colorectal cancers being diagnosed.”

The pattern could help connect the growing incidence among younger adults with the increases now appearing in older populations. American Cancer Society statistics show that colorectal cancer incidence among people ages 20 to 49 rose approximately 3% annually from 2013 to 2022.

“There are many unique challenges when you’re diagnosed with cancer at a young age, so it’s important that we shine a spotlight on that problem,” Murphy said. “But in terms of understanding the root cause, I think our study shows that we can consider that whatever might be causing colorectal cancer in younger people is now also causing it to increase in older people as well.”

The steepest increase occurred among adults ages 45 to 49, whose colorectal cancer incidence rose by 12.76% annually between 2019 and 2023. Researchers believe this unusually rapid increase largely reflects expanded screening following a change in national guidelines that lowered the recommended starting age from 50 to 45.

Screening can temporarily raise reported incidence by detecting cancers that were already present but had not yet been diagnosed. “That means that screening is doing as we intend it to do — it’s catching cancer earlier,” Murphy said.

However, increased screening cannot fully account for the broader rise across age groups, especially among older adults who already have high screening participation. The researchers also examined whether disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic contributed to the trend. The increase persisted even when data from 2020 and 2021 were excluded, and screening activity had largely returned to pre-pandemic levels by January 2021. These findings suggest that pandemic-related delays are unlikely to explain the overall increase observed in 2023.

The underlying causes of the rising incidence remain uncertain. Researchers are now examining whether exposure to chemicals in the environment could contribute to colorectal cancer risk, although no specific exposure has been established as responsible for the observed trends.

“These results motivate us to understand reasons for this rise, so risk factors can be identified and possibly reduced,” said Sonia Kupfer, MD, director of the Gastrointestinal Cancer Risk and Prevention Clinic at UChicago Medicine.

Murphy is collaborating with Xin Hu, PhD, a toxicologist at Emory University, to investigate thousands of environmental chemical exposures using small blood samples. This approach will help identify substances that may warrant closer examination.

The researchers then plan to work with Kupfer to study selected chemicals using colon organoids, laboratory-grown models that reproduce some features of human colon tissue. These models will allow scientists to investigate how particular exposures affect colon cells and potentially identify biological processes involved in cancer development.

Until the causes become clearer, colorectal cancer screening remains one of the most effective ways to identify the disease early and prevent deaths. Current national guidelines recommend routine screening for adults at average risk beginning at age 45 and continuing through age 75. Screening decisions outside that range should be individualized based on factors such as family history, symptoms, and other medical conditions.

Colonoscopy remains the gold standard because it can both detect cancer and remove precancerous polyps. Other screening methods include home-based stool tests and newer blood-based tests, although their capabilities and recommended testing intervals differ.

“There are several ways to do screening that should be discussed with a clinician, so people know what the different screening tests are intended for and how often these need to be repeated,” Kupfer added.

For adults younger than 45 who do not qualify for routine screening, recognizing possible warning signs is particularly important. Blood in the stool, persistent changes in bowel habits, and unexplained weight loss should prompt medical evaluation, even in people without a known family history or genetic risk.

The research team also recommends continued monitoring of national cancer registry data as new figures become available. If the upward trend persists as large generations of Americans grow older, healthcare systems may face increasing demand for cancer treatment, follow-up care, and long-term support for survivors.

Reference: “Colorectal Cancer among U.S. Adults, 2000–2023” by Caitlin C. Murphy, Folasade P. May, Sonia S. Kupfer, Xin Hu and Samir Gupta, 27 October 2026, NEJM Evidence. DOI: 10.1056/EVIDpha2600217

The study was supported by funding from the National Institutes of Health and the Eli and Edythe Broad Center of Regenerative Medicine and Stem Cell Research Ablon Scholars Program.

Social Security Trustees Project 22% Benefit Cut in 2032. This Change Would Cut Benefits by Only 17% Instead

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There's a potentially less bad outcome, but it's not so clear-cut.

There are millions of older Americans today who rely on Social Security for retirement income. And without those benefits, many would be unable to afford basic living expenses.

Unfortunately, Social Security is facing a major financial crisis that could come to a head in just a few years. In fact, benefit cuts are something all retirees might need to brace for.

One potential solution could reduce the extent to which Social Security has to cut benefits. But it's not guaranteed to happen.

The reason Social Security faces benefit cuts boils down to a shrinking workforce. The program's main source of revenue is payroll taxes. But as older workers retire and a smaller share of replacement workers enter the labor force, Social Security will be under strain. The program is not expected to have sufficient incoming tax revenue to keep up with scheduled benefits once there's no trust fund to tap.

The Social Security Trustees, in their most recent report, projected that the program's Old-Age and Survivors Insurance (OASI) Trust Fund would run out of money by the fourth quarter of 2032. Once that happens, Social Security may face a 22% benefit cut.

The OASI Trust Fund is where Social Security's retirement benefits are paid from. But Social Security has another trust fund called the Disability Insurance (DI) Trust Fund, which pays disability benefits.

If the two trust funds were to be combined, Social Security would be able to pay retirement benefits in full for almost an extra two years. And from there, those benefits would only face a 17% cut.

The problem, though, is that Social Security can't simply combine the two funds. Congress would need to vote for that change.

Also, combining the OASI and DI funds doesn't prevent benefit cuts. It simply reduces them while slightly shifting the timeline.

It also puts the DI Trust Fund at risk of depletion much sooner than it would otherwise run out. The current projection by the Trustees indicates the DI Trust Fund could last at least through 2099 if left alone.

Of course, the main reason Congress might hesitate to combine the two Social Security trust funds is that a 17% benefit cut would still be catastrophic to millions of older Americans, not to mention future retirees. There's a good chance that if lawmakers make a change, they'll focus on one that can prevent Social Security cuts entirely.
